#167. What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

One of the Artificial Intelligence (AI) industry's most important figures has been accused of having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy.

US intelligence officials believed that Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, who had a close relationship with Nvidia chief executive and co-founder Jensen Huang, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report.

Jensen Huang admitted, external to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

Nvidia, one of the most valuable companies in the world, has opened an independent investigation into the affair.

It said it does not believe US national security had been compromised - and added that Jensen Huang is committed to continuing to lead the company.

Here is what we know about the affair and why it matters.

Jensen Huang (left) and Alice Huang - also known as Huang Le - at an event

US officials believed that Alice Huang had been recruited to spy by China's top intelligence agency, the New York Times reported, external. Her current whereabouts are unknown.

Alice Huang, no relation to Jensen Huang, was a top executive at Singapore-based data centre firm Megaspeed between March and November 2023.

The company had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China. Nvidia said this was investigated and debunked.

However, Megaspeed is under investigation by the Singapore government regarding previous allegations that the company was circumventing US sanctions on the export of Nvidia's high-end chips.

The BBC has contacted Megaspeed for comment.

Surveillance reportedly revealed communications between Alice Huang and an American citizen, who was subsequently identified as Jensen Huang.

It remains unclear what, if any, intelligence Alice Huang â who is also known as Huang Le â allegedly gathered and passed to Beijing.

When the BBC asked Nvidia if the company was aware of intelligence concerns, the company said it was not.

"We trust that if we were the target of a genuine threat, the US government would have notified us," an Nvidia spokesperson told the BBC.

China has rejected the spying allegations.

Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun said: "The allegations about Chinese spies are entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China."

There were media reports in October that US President Donald Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence alleging Alice Huang was spying on China's behalf.

On whether he wished he had "acted on the intelligence", Trump told journalists: "No, I don't think so. I think he's [Jensen Huang] an honourable guy."

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, refused to comment when asked about Jensen Huang.

In spring this year, the secretary of state reportedly oversaw the drafting of a memo that warned the Trump administration about Jensen Huang's relationship with the Chinese businesswoman.

Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Nvidia hires major New York law firm to investigate Jensen Huang affair

Nvidia is at the heart of US and China's AI race, which is why the billionaire boss's affair has sparked national security fears among American lawmakers.

Trump has said "whoever wins AI wins", and the concern is that if China gains the upper hand in AI, it will supercharge the country's military and surveillance capabilities.

The US advantage is in large part because of companies like Nvidia, working with producers such as the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC), which makes more than 90% of the world's most advanced chips.

Since 2022, the US has used various export controls to keep the top tech out of China, which it says helps retain America's competitive edge and protects its national security.

This means companies like Nvidia have been largely locked out of the world's most lucrative market, with Jensen Huang once saying that Nvidia's share of the Chinese market had gone from around 95% to effectively zero.

Jensen Huang has engaged in shuttle diplomacy between Washington and Beijing, hoping to find a US-approved way back in, and shaping US policy on China along the way.

The revelations may explain why Jensen Huang has failed to bring home the biggest prize of all to Nvidia: the Trump administration's blessing to sell all but its most advanced chips to China.

Nvidia boss Jensen Huang and US President Donald Trump at the White House

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Jensen Huang was nine years old when his family moved to the West Coast of the US. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

He worked at US chip firm AMD before co-founding Nvidia. Known as "Daddy Huang" in Taiwan and once lauded as the "Taylor Swift of tech", he has a reputation as a workaholic with incredibly high standards for his staff.

He has an estimated fortune of $155bn (Â£115bn), according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on the Jensen Huang, told the BBC, adding that he was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

The Nvidia co-founder has a famously close relationship with Trump. At the recent AI summit at the White House, where 42 tech executives were present, the president was flanked by Elon Musk and Jensen Huang.

Trump said he spoke with Jensen Huang after reports emerged about Alice Huang and her alledged links to the Chinese state and asked him: "'Did you have any idea she was a spy?

"He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," said Trump.

Nvidia has launched an independent investigation into the affair and has hired New York law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz.

It will be led by Joon Kim, a former US Attorney for New York's Southern District who is now at litigation partner at the law firm.

US President Donald Trump said on Monday that the FBI was also investigating the affair. The FBI declined to comment further when contacted by the BBC.

Jensen Huang has also been asked to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees on 18 November.

Members of the US House Foreign Affairs Committee asked the Nvidia chief executive to appear to explain his interactions with Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's security protocols around espionage.

Jensen Huang has yet to say he will comply, a person familiar with the committee's demand told the BBC.

There is no clear successor for Jensen Huang at Nvidia

Founded by Jensen Huang and fellow electrical engineers Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem in 1993, the company was originally known for chips that process graphics, particularly for computer games.

Eventually it changed focus to AI, a field it now dominates.

Jensen Huang rescued Nvidia from bankruptcy on more than one occasion, and took risks on AI a long time before the rest of the world did, effectively building the infrastructure from scratch.

Huang bet on AI at a time when "there were no customers for the stuff he was building", Witt told the BBC.

Every notable tech company building AI tools and infrastructure like Amazon, Google and Microsoft now use Nvidia chips.

Currently worth about $4tn, Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies in the world.

For now, the firm's board appears unlikely to remove Jensen Huang, saying on Sunday they have "confidence in Jensen and his leadership team".

Sources tell the BBC his removal is unlikely because there is so much money at stake.

There is no public succession plan for the 63-year-old. He has often said he hopes to continue in the role for decades, and Bloomberg reports he has created an informal leadership structure around him unlike a traditional company's hierarchy.

His children, Spencer and Madison, and son-in-law Nico Caprez work at Nvidia in senior roles.

Lori Huang and her husband at a state dinner by Trump for his Chinese President Xi Jinping

Jensen Huang met his future wife Lori Mills at Oregon State University while studying electrical engineering in the early 1980s. The pair were lab partners.

They married in 1985 and have two children, Madison and Spencer.

In 2007, the couple founded the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Foundation. The charitable fund has seen its assets surge in recent years, driven by the soaring value of Nvidia shares, and is estimated to be worth more than $9bn.

Its donations include tens of millions of dollars to engineering programmes at Oregon State and Stanford University.

Witt told the BBC that Jensen Huang has gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, adding: "That's very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," he added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

Additional reporting by Tom McArthur in London, Ayeshea Perera and Tessa Wong in Singapore and Helen Davidson in Sydney

#164. Qantas to outsource 850 local jobs with most roles moving to India

Qantas CEO Vanessa Hudson apologised for the "hard news" of 850 job cuts

Qantas will outsource 850 local corporate jobs to consulting giant Accenture with most roles moving to India as the airline fast-tracks AI investment.

In an internal email to staff on Tuesday, the Australian company's CEO Vanessa Hudson said "this is hard news for some and I'm sorry for the uncertainty it brings".

The job cuts will begin mid-2027, she said, and start with finance, procurement, people and marketing roles shifting "mostly" to teams in India and "some in Australia" as the firm seeks to modernise its IT systems.

Hudson said the "scale and speed of change in technology and AI is something none of us has seen in our lifetime", and it was in talks with unions and staff on the plans.

The move comes days after the airline provided a market update, flagging fare increases and flight cuts amid higher fuel costs.

On its new multi-year deal with Accenture, Hudson said it was about "modernising" the airline's IT systems to become "more efficient".

Qantas spends more than AU$1bn (US$691m; Â£514m) a year on technology, she said, and the company's back-end systems needed to be upgraded.

"Like every airline, we run on technology built up over decades for a different era, including more than 1,000 legacy applications that were never designed to work together," she said.

The deal with Accenture means "we will redesign and simplify parts of how our corporate functions work and reduce duplication", Hudson said, so staff can "spend more time on work that creates value for our customers and the business".

The company said that "in time", the work that Accenture takes over will be "transformed further with automation and AI".

Staff affected by the job cuts will be redeployed "where possible", the firm added, including new roles in Australia.

Qantas - which employs about 28,000 staff in Australia and another 4,000 globally - said thousands of its staff already use AI in their daily work.

The BBC has contacted the Australian Services Union for comment.

#161. Poundland unable to buy new stock as fears over collapse grow

Poundland is unable to buy new stock to fill its shelves after being blocked by its owners from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

The discount chain is on the brink of collapse with one insider describing it as "running on fumes". It has until Thursday to secure a rescue deal.

About Â£50m has been frozen by its American owner Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning its 600 shops are running out of stock.

Pressure is building on Gordon Brothers, which bought Poundland for Â£1 last June, after the owner rejected a takeover bid that could have saved the thousands of jobs currently at risk. Gordon Brothers has been approached for comment.

Poundland has filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, which protects it from its creditors until Thursday.

Access to funds was limited because of a tension between Gordon Brothers' dual role as both creditor and shareholder, a source said, but added they expected a relaxation of funds in the next two days.

Last week, Poundland boss Barry Williams told staff like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4% last week, which included Halloween, but said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He said the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year where the business made losses of Â£68m.

The latest annual accounts have yet to be audited.

Burnham 'ready to support' Poundland staff as retailer faces collapse

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture of the business, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It said it had provided significant funding to support the company's ability to trade since it acquired the business.

It has also made Â£18m, from a combination of debt interest payments and other fees and charges.

On Wednesday, executives from Gordon Brothers will appear before the Business Select Committee with MPs probing whether they are genuinely pursuing a solvent rescue of the business, which has 11,000 employees, and whether every realistic step is being taken to protect jobs and stores.

The session is being held in private given commercially sensitive discussions are still under way with bidders.

On Thursday, the 10-day moratorium to protect Poundland from its creditors expires, but if there is a prospect of a solvent deal, the deadline could be extended.

If not, Poundland is set to collapse into administration and could be broken up.

A source familiar with the sales process said London-based Xyra Group was making "significant progress" in its last minute bid to buy Poundland and could now be the front runner.

An extension of Thursday's deadline now seems an increasingly likely scenario. But such a case would be difficult to put forward if Gordon Brothers continues to block funding.

One observer remarked "if Gordon Brothers is going in front of that committee, you don't want to be giving the message we're pulling up the drawbridge".

Even if a deal is agreed to sell the business as a whole, there is no guarantee a new owner would not reduce the number of stores, but suppliers to Poundland would prefer that outcome to hundreds of sites being sold off to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up stores which could mean continuing employment for many workers.

The central issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland trading as a national chain.

#160. Inside India newsletter: Trump's poll setback fuels hope for U.S.-India trade deal by year end

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from India.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

After months of being weighed down by high energy prices, trade uncertainty and foreign investor exodus, India has had plenty to celebrate this Diwali. Foreign investors returned to its market in November and, now, it seems the long-awaited U.S.-India trade deal could be around the corner.

Experts say that the two sides are closer to signing on the dotted line following the U.S. midterm election results than they were before.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

The U.S.-India trade deal, which has been riddled with uncertainty since February, is likely to get a final push to the finish line, thanks to U.S. President Donald Trump's electoral setback in the midterm polls.

Experts told CNBC that Trump could lean toward closing some of the pending trade deals to promote them as policy achievements to the American public, and the pact with New Delhi is a "prime candidate."

They added that Trump and Indian Prime Minister Modi are likely to sign the trade pact when they meet in December for the G20 leaders meeting, as negotiations have largely concluded.

The U.S.-India trade talks "are far along and there seems to be a gentlemen's agreement between Trump and Modi" that the deal should be concluded when the two meet at Mar-a-Lago in December, Mark Linscott, former assistant U.S. trade representative and senior advisor at the U.S.-India Strategic Partnership Forum, told CNBC.

Trump has used tariffs as a pressure tactic to clinch trade deals with several countries, even as that approach has drawn the ire of his political opponents who have flagged that export levies have increased costs for American households. Cost of living was the top concern for American voters in the midterms.

"The election was a verdict on the cost of living, and tariffs are now a liability the President cannot defend on the numbers," Ronak D. Desai, visiting fellow at Hoover Institution of Stanford University, told CNBC.

"Congress will not hand him an affordability win, but a trade deal is one he can announce on his own," Desai said, adding that an announcement of a deal with India is "more likely than it was before the election."

India's commerce and external affairs ministries and the U.S. Trade Representative's Office did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

When asked about how U.S. midterm election results will reshape the ties with New Delhi, a spokesperson for India's Ministry of External Affairs on Tuesday said that the relationship between the two countries "enjoys bipartisan support in the United States."

Trump insists Americans do not share the burden of his tariffs, but according to estimates from Tax Foundation, a nonprofit, the duties raised the average U.S. household's tax burden by $820 in 2026 and $1,000 in 2025.

As a result, the Democrats, within days of taking control of both the houses of Congress are preparing to challenge Trump's signature tariff policy. A trade deal at this time could be seen as a validation of Trump's tariffs-led approach to business.

"The president may lean more into getting deals done – seeing them as policy achievements that he can promote to the American public," Atman Trivedi, partner at Washington-based DGA Albright Stonebridge Group, told CNBC. He added that the Trump administration would like to get a deal done with India by the G20 leaders' summit in December.

While American voters are more affected by import duties on Canadian goods, experts said that a trade deal with India could blunt criticism about tariffs as an effective bargaining chip.

In February, Trump announced a deal with India, claiming that New Delhi had agreed to "Buy American" at a much higher level after the U.S. agreed to slash tariffs to 18%. Trump had earlier imposed a 25% tariff on imports from India, along with an additional 25% duty on goods from New Delhi for buying Russian oil.

But that deal has since been awaiting final approval, especially after the Supreme Court struck down Trump tariffs, giving India space to demand preferential tariffs that give it an edge over competitors.

While American and Indian officials at the start of October said that the negotiations had reached an impasse, Trump on Oct. 20 said that the two sides were "pretty close" to a final deal. As per Indian media reports, the country's Commerce Minister Piyush Goyal confirmed that discussions on the trade deal had largely "concluded."

"We have been hearing for weeks that a deal is close to be settled," Aryan D'Rozario, associate fellow and chair on India and Emerging Asia Economics at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, told CNBC, adding that Modi's visit to the G20 summit in December "will provide the perfect stage to formalize an agreement."

India as an AI hedge? The country's stocks could see robust inflows as markets globally tankThe Indian stock market is emerging as an attractive hedge as investors seek refuge from a brutal sell-off in the artificial intelligence trade, making it a strong contender for global capital. If global investors "neutralize" their underweight positions on India, it could lead to foreign inflows of $115 billion, as per JPMorgan.

India billionaire Adani's firm buys Dutch firm Evos to gain foothold in EuropeAdani Ports and Special Economic Zone, India's largest port operator, will acquire Netherlands-headquartered Evos from Igneo Infrastructure Partners in an all-cash deal at an enterprise value of 2.56 billion euros.

#159. Civil servant who secretly held five jobs found guilty of fraud

Civil servant Kashim Chowdhury juggled multiple public sector jobs

A civil servant who secretly held multiple public sector jobs on full-time salaries has been found guilty of nine counts of fraud.

Kashim Chowdhury, from east London, had five government jobs that overlapped in the spring of 2023. He fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money.

The prosecution accused the 56-year-old of ''deliberate, intentional dishonesty'' and of ''exploiting the system''.

After seven hours of deliberation, the jury unanimously found Chowdhury guilty of eight counts of fraud by false representation and one count of fraud by a failure to disclose.

The judge said Chowdhury faced a ''very significant custodial sentence''. He added he was refusing bail because of the significance of the crime and for Chowdhury's own wellbeing.

"This was sustained and deliberate dishonesty," said Sarah Melo, specialist prosecutor at the CPS Serious Economic Organised Crime and International Directorate.

"He knew he could not legitimately hold multiple full-time positions at the same time, but continued to conceal what he was doing."

A Defra spokesperson said Chowdury "abused the trust placed in him as a public servant", adding that it would use all available avenues to recover the proceeds of Chowdhury's offending.

The judge said Chowdhury faced a ''very significant custodial sentence''

Between 2020 and 2023 Chowdhury had eight full-time public sector roles that overlapped and earned him Â£350,000 in total.

The prosecution told the court that on application forms he provided inaccurate information about his employment status and failed to disclose to government departments that he was already a civil servant. Once he secured the roles he didn't tell his employers he was working elsewhere.

He was allowed to work entirely from home because he told his employers he was caring for his mother, whom he lived with in east London. She had dementia and was diagnosed with terminal cancer in 2023.

How a civil servant got away with juggling five jobs for so long

But rather than reduce his workload to look after her, he kept taking on new jobs. In the spring of 2023, when his mother was a few months away from death, he had jobs at the Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs, the Department of Health and Social Care, the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), the Department for Business and Trade, the Food Standards Agency and the Home Office.

During this period he was contracted to work 183 hours a week - more than the number of hours that exist in seven days.

Chowdhury said he had taken on so many roles because he feared losing his livelihood after experiencing insecure employment in the past. He was made redundant from a job at the Department for Education in 2013 and was given a diagnosis of PTSD.

He said he had not been thinking clearly when applying for jobs ''because of my stress, distress and trauma and due to my panic at the time".

But the prosecution barrister, Lucy Organ, told the jury: "He's lied to you in evidence, he's lied to you under oath, which demonstrates the level of deceit he has descended to."

A succession of his managers told the court about his poor performance. Especially damning was the evidence given by Dan McCartney who managed Chowdhury at Defra.

He said he had to "effectively tell him what to do the whole time". McCartney said that during video calls Chowdhury had his camera switched off blaming technical problems. McCartney said this went on for four to five months.

But McCartney said that because Chowdhury was not subject to any formal performance measures at Defra, he was given a permanent job and remained there for two years.

Chowdhury told the jury he had not worn make-up during a work video call

The prosecution argued that he used sick notes to cope with his impossible schedule. In March 2023, he was on sick leave from four of the organisations he worked for. But he kept working at the Home Office and did not tell them he was ill.

His line manager at the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), Aleesha Adams, organised a video call with him in May 2023. By this point he'd been off sick for three months and she wanted to make a plan to get him back to work.

She told the jury that at the start of the call he was coughing a lot and waving tissues up to the camera. She also told the jury that he had been wearing white make-up to try to look ill.

Adams told the court that as the meeting went on the make-up started coming off his forehead. ''He was sweating, it was like rubbing off so it was darker underneath and darker on the neck."

Chowdhury told the jury this was a smear and that he had not been wearing make-up.

After the call, Adams discovered that he had logged into the call using a Defra email address. When she challenged him about it he blamed a technical error.

Adams grew suspicious. ''I believed he was lying,'' she told the jury. She escalated her concerns up the chain at the UKHSA. But his fraudulent behaviour went on for several more months.

The government's National Fraud Initiative flagged suspected fraud to Defra in 2023. Following this, the Defra Investigations Team launched an investigation and as a result, Chowdhury was arrested and interviewed under caution in July 2023. He was dismissed by the civil service in September 2023.

Chowdhury has also been accused by his siblings of bullying their mother into increasing his share of the family home days before she died.

In a case at the High Court, Chowdhury's two brothers and sister allege that he put "undue influence" on Faijun Nessa to sign documents that meant he would inherit half of her home.

The four-storey, six-bedroom home in east London has been valued at Â£2.1m.

His siblings say their mother could not have understood what she was signing because she was "desperately unwell". In January 2023 she was given a terminal diagnosis of stage four metastatic colorectal cancer.

The claimants also allege that Mrs Nessa, who was nearly blind and could not speak English, was unaware of what she was signing because the documents were not translated into her native Bengali at the time they were signed.

Kashim Chowdhury denies the claims. That case is ongoing.

#156. Australia considers criminal penalties for rogue AI in wake of OpenAI hack

PM Anthony Albanese says the law needs to catch up with a "fast-changing world"

Australia plans to create a new AI safety regulator and has flagged criminal penalties for tech companies who do not comply, months after an OpenAI hack.

In September, Australia's Prime Minister Anthony Albanese revealed that a rogue OpenAI agent had "infiltrated" a government health website and other systems, and criticised the company for taking months to tell them about the hack.

On Tuesday, he released the 18 recommendations made after a "rapid review" into the breaches including new safety and security standards for AI companies such as mandatory reporting of hacks.

AI brings opportunities, he said, but Australia must prepare for the "new risks" from this "rapidly advancing technology".

"The law needs to get ahead of and, in some cases, make sure that it catches up with, what is a very fast changing world," Albanese said.

One of the recommendations suggests changes to criminal law to have a standalone "failure to prevent" offence .

This would criminalise instances where a company develops "an AI model that engages in harmful conduct" and "fails to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm".

The review also recommended "mandatory, time-bound AI safety reporting" of hacks.

This point addresses what Albanese had earlier called his "disappointment" in the months-long delay for OpenAI to tell his government about the breach.

In June, a statistics portal containing "non-sensitive" data from Australia's universal healthcare scheme Medicare was hacked by a rogue OpenAI agent.

OpenAI said it only learnt of the breach in August while reviewing "misaligned model activity" and emailed a general inbox of an Australian government agency on 10 September.

It took several more days before the email was escalated to Australia's cybersecurity centre, with a message then sent to a government minister ahead of the prime minister being told.

Albanese said he spoke to OpenAI boss Sam Altman and raised "Australia's extreme concern about this incident", with Altman acknowledging there were "issues with protocols" at OpenAI.

Days after the hacks were revealed, OpenAI delayed its rollout of a new AI model, citing safety concerns.

Why did an OpenAI system hack Australia's health system - and can it be stopped in the future?

OpenAI scraps rollout of new model over safety concerns

#154. Bouncing Wynn Shares might be primed for the 'wheel strategy'

In Barry Levinson's classic film Rain Man, Dustin Hoffman's character leans over to his brother in the middle of a blackjack hand with a simple piece of advice: bet two for good, one for bad.

In the case of Wynn Resorts shares, it might be time to bet two for good.

Steve Wynn hasn't exactly been on a winning streak. No longer running his namesake company, he finally sold his 50 Central Park South penthouse after years on the market. WYNN shares haven't really been on a winning streak either. The stock is down more than 27% year-to-date as I write this. It was down more than 46% from the 2025 highs within the past couple of weeks. But it has bounced a bit since then, and perhaps the tables are turning.

Fundamentally, the business is demonstrating exceptional strength, particularly in the United States. Wynn's Las Vegas EBITDA margin has actually surpassed that of its Macau resorts. Recognizing this, management has accelerated capital spending in Las Vegas, where the company's profit has doubled from pre-pandemic levels. Meanwhile, the Asian market is also showing signs of life. Strategic property upgrades in Macau are successfully drawing lucrative premium mass play, which is directly supporting the company's ambitious expansion plans for the Enclave at Wynn Palace.

From a valuation standpoint, WYNN is trading very close to its five-year historical averages across key metrics, including P/E, EV/EBITDA (currently around 9x to 10x), and Price-to-Book Value. Although this represents a slight premium compared to the broader gaming industry mean, Wynn's justifies a premium. (The Encore, the newer of the two adjacent Las Vegas properties, is one of my preferred hotels on "The Strip"). Technically, the stock has admittedly been in a downtrend, but this recent sharp bounce off the late October lows may indicate the run is over. Several technical indicators—specifically the MACD, Fear & Greed Index, Commodity Channel Index (CCI), and several key Moving Averages—are now aligned, suggesting it may be time to go long.

With the next earnings report not scheduled until February, the stock has a clear runway free of binary fundamental catalysts, yet implied volatility remains surprisingly high. This environment makes buying options expensive and short premium strategies more attractive.

Rather than betting on the roulette wheel, traders should look once again to "the wheel" strategy. Something I discussed earlier this week on United Rentals. The strategy? Step one, sell cash-covered puts below the current market price to collect that inflated premium. If WYNN stays above your strike price through expiration, you keep the cash and repeat the process. If the stock dips and you are assigned, you acquire shares at a discount. Once assigned, you move to step two: selling covered calls against your new stock position to generate continuous income until the shares are eventually called away, after which you revert back to step one and play again. A methodical, high-probability way to get long a world-class operator while getting paid to wait.

#152. Californians reject wealth tax for billionaires

California voters have voted to reject a wealth tax on its billionaires.

The ballot proposal would have levied a one-time tax on the state's mega wealthy to help fund healthcare. It took nearly a week of counting votes after the midterm election to determine a projection on the measure.

There are about 200 billionaires in the state, including Meta's Mark Zuckerberg, reality television star Kim Kardashian and media mogul Oprah Winfrey.

Proposition 40 targeted Californians with a wealth of more than $1.1bn (Â£800m) with a 5% tax on their total wealth, including assets such as shares as well as income. It failed amid divisions among Democrats and labour unions.

Are California voters about to impose 'world's first' tax on billionaires?

Had it passed, experts believe it would have been the world's first wealth tax targeting billionaires.

The tax would have raised about $100bn to make up for federal funding cuts to healthcare in California as a result of President Donald Trump's sweeping tax and spending bill.

Those who backed the measure had argued it would help ensure that the ultra-wealthy were contributing their fair share and helping backfill gaps in the state's budget.

The wealth tax faced fierce opposition, including from Google co-founder Sergey Brin, as well as California Governor Gavin Newsom and labour unions.

Newsom, whose tenure as governor is coming to an end, had expressed concerns that the measure could cause the wealthy to leave the state and drive out their crucial income tax revenue.

Some billionaire critics of the measure spent millions trying to crush it, with a handful even moving out of the state to evade the possible tax. Brin gave more than $100m to a political organisation funding ballot measures that could block the tax, according to the Los Angeles Times.

Dave Regan, head of the union that led the charge for the measure, said on election night that the billionaires' efforts to block the tax created "a lot of confusion and a lot of second-guessing among people who really in their hearts know what the right the thing to do is".

Regan, who is president of Service Employees International Union-United Healthcare Workers West, pledged to seek a billionaire tax once more in 2028.

The measure had odds stacked against it, with two other propositions on the ballot that would have effectively reversed it, even if it passed.

California, home to some 23 million registered voters, has the largest economy in the country and boasts the fourth largest global economy. It is home to one of the highest concentrations of billionaires in the world, but also has one of the highest wealth gaps.

#151. Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

#149. Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang, 63, has been called the 'Taylor Swift of tech' in the US

The tech world has spent the day digesting the extraordinary news that Jensen Huang - the charismatic, leather-jacket wearing chief executive of the multi-trillion dollar firm Nvidia - is being investigated by his own company over an extramarital affair with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy.

None of us had that on our bingo cards for 2026.

Huang's alleged romantic affair with Alice Huang (no relation), the former CEO of a Singapore-based data centre company, has cast ripples far beyond his immediate family - undoubtedly on an emotional rollercoaster of their own - taking us to a crossroads at which technology, geopolitics and national security intersect.

In a nutshell, this is about the bitter rivalry between the US and China, as well as whether Jensen Huang has betrayed the US as well as his wife.

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

The US is locked in a fierce race with China to develop the best AI models.

"Whoever wins AI, wins," as US President Donald Trump puts it. He thinks that the country with the more dominant AI technology will benefit the most from it and, ultimately, will be the most powerful in the world. He may well be right.

Nvidia happens to make the best chips in the business for AI â so much so that it has been overwhelmed with demand.

It sets its prices accordingly: a single unit of the firm's latest kit for data centres starts at Â£37,000 ($50,000). A medium or large site might have hundreds of them.

The US administration initially slapped an export ban on Nvidia's more recent devices to prevent China from taking advantage of their superior capabilities. It has since softened the rules a little.

But there are suggestions that Alice Huang's company, Megaspeed, may have been involved in sneaking them into the country while she was in charge. Bloomberg reported that the firm bought billions of dollars worth of Nvidia tech in November 2025. Megaspeed denies that it was used illegitimately.

For his part, Jensen Huang has lobbied heavily for the export rules between the two countries to be relaxed, arguing against the weight of general opinion that the AI race doesn't matter and that it would be no bad thing for China to become dependent on US tech.

"President Trump wants American to win and President Xi wants China to win, and it's possible for both of them to do so," he told the BBC last year.

I think it's safe to assume that neither country sees it that way.

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

The difficult questions Nvidia now has to try to answer include whether Alice gained special access to the firm's latest technology, either deliberately or not, via Jensen - and, if so, whether she shared it with Beijing or others.

We don't know when their affair began or ended, but there are numerous photos and videos of Jensen and Alice together at events. It is feasible that there were moments when they spent quite a lot of time together and Jensen has talked about always having an eye on his work.

China denies that she was engaged in espionage.

Interest in economic espionage is neither new nor unique to China. But many Western countries say that China does it on a grand scale, using a combination of technology tools and agents embedded in various organisations - including private companies.

China having access to significant enough numbers of Nvidia's chips to make use of them, or stripping them down and learning how to make its own versions, would infuriate the Trump administration, which is trying to keep its AI technology close to its chest.

Some models have even been withheld from the UK-based AI Safety Institute on the grounds that they are potentially too dangerous to be shared. The US does not want anyone having a headstart or misusing its innovations.

Another potential bombshell lies in the form of any national security information to which Jensen Huang may have been privy.

He is a man operating at the very top of the US elite - one of a handful of people who could probably walk into the White House unannounced and have tea with Trump. He has influence, he has access and he has powerful friends. It's quite possible that secrets were shared with him.

Nvidia says there's no evidence so far that he passed anything on and the firm has launched an internal investigation. Huang says he will co-operate and wants to keep his job - though if nothing else, he has broken Nvidia's own code of conduct, which states that company members should not have relationships with clients.

At present, he has the backing of both the Nvidia board and Trump, who described him as "honourable" and speculated that there must have been a reason for what had transpired. In other words, he's not throwing his tech boss buddy under the bus.

Nvidia's share price has fallen but has not tanked spectacularly, suggesting shareholders are not abandoning the company under his stewardship in their droves either.

An AI industry with a weakened Nvidia would undoubtedly be a slower one.

And there isn't really a natural successor waiting in the wings to replace Jensen were he forced out.

Huang has run Nvidia for more than 30 years - to many, he is Nvidia. He has steered it through multiple crises and it was his decision for the firm to focus on AI before it was trendy.

He is known to be a demanding boss who expects staff to work long hours and to high standards. There are stories about him calling all-staff meetings to tell off individuals who had made mistakes. He has said this was to prevent others making the same errors â but you can only imagine how it felt for those on the receiving end.

While he awaits the conclusion of the investigation into his actions, Huang must hope that, despite not tolerating the mistakes of his colleagues in the past, they will be gracious about his own failings.

And he must also live with the irony that he was potentially caught out not by a sophisticated high-tech sting, but by the oldest trick in the book: a honey trap.

#147. Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Hundreds of billions of dollars have been wiped off the value of Nvidia after the US chipmaker announced an independent investigation would be carried out into its chief executive's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The tech firm saw its shares fall by about 4% by close of trade on Monday after Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair.

Nvidia's probe came after reports that the US believed the Nvidia boss had been targeted in an espionage campaign by China.

However, China has rejected the claims, with its foreign ministry saying allegations about Chinese spies were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised, but a statement by the firm's board of directors said it would begin an investigation in "light of recent reporting that founder and chief executive Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report, external.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Jensen Huang admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children. He added he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation.

The Nvidia co-founder has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

Members of the US House Foreign Affairs Committee last week asked Jensen Huang to appear for a closed-door briefing on his interactions with Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's security protocols around espionage.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's [China's] access to sensitive US technology and information," congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar, and Rick Crawford wrote in a letter.

"It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which US national security has been placed at risk."

After Nvidia's investigation was announced, other big tech bosses, including Elon Musk, came out in support of Jensen Huang to continue leading the firm.

US President Donald Trump said the Nvidia boss was an "honourable guy".

However, the news sparked a sell-off in Nvidia's shares, with about $250bn (Â£190bn) wiped of the company's market value.

While Nvidia shares fell, the US stock market opened the week on a positive note after last week's turmoil, with Nvidia's rival chipmakers benefiting.

But analysts suggested the hit to the company would be short-lived. Victoria Fernandez, chief market strategist at Crossmark Global Investments said investors would not abandon Nvidia, because the chipmaker was so important to the AI industry.

"Nvidia is the core of this AI trade, and without them, all these other pieces start to fall apart because the financing's not there," she told the BBC.

Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia, which is central to making the hardware for emerging AI technologies, with two other engineers in 1993.

Its chips were initially designed for gaming, but he decided to turn its focus on AI development, turning it into the world's second most valuable company.

A key concern for investors going forward is what would happen to Nvidia if Jensen Huang was removed from the company, as Fernandez said there was "no natural successor" to the chief executive.

Jensen Huang, unlike other founders in the tech industry, is vulnerable to any move by Nvidia's board of directors to oust him.

He owns only around 3.5% of the company, and Nvidia operates on a single class voting structure, meaning his stake would not be enough to act as a veto power over any board decision.

By contrast, many tech founders who lead their companies, including Mark Zuckerberg of Meta and Musk of Tesla and SpaceX, hold significant percentages of multiple types of stock in their companies.

Fernandez said she does not believe the board will remove Jensen Huang as chief executive, unless there is glaring evidence of wrongdoing related to national security or intellectual property, "but I bet they start bringing someone up as his number two, to have there for the future."

The modest fall in its shares mean investors believe it is unlikely he will step down or be removed as chief executive, but that the outcome of the investigation will be closely watched.

Even people who do not directly own shares in Nvidia can be affected by rises and falls in its value. Pensions or savings invested in pots which have stakes in the company through index tracking funds can be impacted, for example.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia chips have been a major focus of the AI race between the US and China and the White House has previously imposed controls on where its advanced products can be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted by Washington over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Jensen Huang has been against restricting the sale of Nvidia's chips to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Additional reporter by Samira Hussain, North America business correspondent.

#145. Social Security could pay full benefits until 2059 if taxable payroll kept pace with GDP: AARP report

Social Security's taxable payroll, which provides most of the revenue for the program that pays monthly benefits to millions of Americans, has fallen as a share of gross domestic product over the past four decades, according to a new report from the AARP Public Policy Institute.

Had payroll tax revenue kept up with GDP, the program could pay full benefits until 2059, according to the research — an additional 25 years beyond the latest projected depletion date of 2034 for the program's combined funds. The AARP Public Policy Institute provides policy research, analysis and development at AARP, a nonprofit, nonpartisan organization that represents Americans ages 50 and over.

"Social Security's finances have weakened not simply because America has grown older, but because the economy has changed, while the program's revenue system has not," Richard Johnson, vice president of financial security at the AARP Public Policy Institute, said during a Tuesday press briefing on the research.

"America's economy is larger and more productive than it was four decades ago, but Social Security is receiving support from a smaller share of that economy," Johnson said.

Social Security's trustees projected in June that the program's combined retirement and disability trust funds may be depleted in the third quarter of 2034. Unless Congress takes action, 83% of scheduled benefits will be payable at that time, according to the trustees' projections.

The trustees' report also included an earlier projected depletion date for Social Security's retirement trust fund — the fourth quarter of 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable. However, the AARP said it based its analysis on the later 2034 date because Congress has shifted money between the funds in the past.

The program's current shortfall is worse than lawmakers had anticipated in 1983, when the last major Social Security reforms were enacted.

Rising income inequality means the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings — a percentage lawmakers expected to remain constant when they made the 1983 reforms, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized there, according to the institute.

AARP points to an aging population as another reason for Social Security's funding challenges. The worker-to-beneficiary ratio will fall from 3.4 to 2.4 between 2000 and 2030. Falling fertility rates are also shrinking the worker-to-beneficiary ratio.

Shifting income patterns have also meant that a smaller share of U.S. GDP is subject to the Social Security payroll tax. Taxable payroll has fallen from 41% of GDP in 1983 to 34% as of 2025, a level Social Security's trustees project will remain, according to the AARP.

AARP attributes the decline to a shift from wages toward business and investment income, which generally isn't subject to payroll taxes. Automation has reduced demand for workers, and pass-through businesses allow some owners to report compensation as profits rather than wages, according to the report.

Social Security would have collected $10.8 trillion in additional funding between 1984 and 2034 if taxable payroll had stayed at 41% of GDP, according to the AARP. That figure is in inflation-adjusted 2025 dollars.

In 2026, up to $184,500 in wages are subject to the Social Security payroll tax.

Payroll taxes accounted for 91% of Social Security's revenues in 2025, according to AARP's research, while 5% came from interest income and 4% was through federal income taxes paid by high-income beneficiaries on their benefits.

To increase Social Security's revenues, lawmakers may consider changes that make high-income taxpayers contribute more to the program while sparing low- to moderate-income taxpayers, according to the AARP report.

"The fix for Social Security isn't cutting earned benefits; it's rebuilding the revenue behind them," Johnson said.

Raising the payroll tax threshold — which is incrementally adjusted each year to keep up with average wages — would not affect most workers, since just 6% of individuals had earnings at or above the cap in 2024, according to the AARP.

In 2027, the Social Security taxable maximum is set to increase to $192,300.

Bills have been introduced in Congress to raise the cap to apply to higher thresholds, such as $250,000 or $400,000, or to eliminate it altogether. Getting rid of the cap would close 67% of the 75-year funding gap if workers do not receive a benefit increase in exchange for those contributions, according to AARP research based on the Social Security Administration's assessments.

Another option: Lawmakers may also shore up the program by taxing income not currently subject to Social Security payroll taxes, according to the AARP, following an increase in business activity through pass-through entities — such as sole proprietorships, partnerships, limited liability companies and S corporations.

Lawmakers could create a new tax on investment income to help fund Social Security's trust funds. A new 12.4% tax on investment income for active S corp. officers and limited partners would close about 25% of Social Security's 75-year financing gap, according to the AARP, which cited research from Social Security's actuaries.

One option not mentioned in the AARP research is raising the payroll tax rate, which is currently 6.2% for workers and employers, or 12.4% combined. The Social Security trustees estimated that an increase in the combined rate to 16.65% beginning in January 2026 would have made the combined program solvent for the 75-year period ending in 2100.

A national survey conducted in February found 62% of individuals would support gradually increasing the payroll tax from 6.2% to 6.5%, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. That poll included 2,509 adults.

The 1983 Social Security reforms included changes to benefits, such as gradually raising the retirement age from 65 to 67, delaying the cost-of-living adjustment by six months and taxing Social Security benefits.

Research shows those changes have reduced lifetime retirement benefits by about 19% for people turning 65 in 2026 compared to what they would have received under the previous rules, Jenn Jones, vice president of financial security and livable communities at the AARP, said during the Tuesday press briefing.

While the AARP has not endorsed any specific benefit changes, Jones said the organization opposes cuts to monthly checks.

"Millions of Americans — we hear from them every day — cannot afford to lose a penny," Jones said.

#144. How a civil servant got away with juggling five jobs for so long

Kashim Chowdhury fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money

Aleesha Adams joined a video call with Kashim Chowdhury in May 2023.

He'd been on sick leave from his job at the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA) for nearly three months and Adams, his line manager, wanted to put a plan in action to ease him back into work.

At the start of the call, Chowdhury was coughing and waving tissues up to the camera in an attempt to show how ill he was. But what really caught Adams' attention was how pale he looked.

She noticed white powder had started separating from his forehead. "He was sweating, it was like rubbing off, so it was darker underneath and darker on the neck," she told his trial.

She claimed he was wearing white make-up to look sick. Something he denied in court.

Little did she know UKHSA was not Chowdhury's only employer.

That month, he was also being paid to work full-time for the Department for the Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra), the Department for Business and Trade, the Food Standards Agency and the Home Office. He was contracted to work 183 hours a week â more than the number of hours in seven days. He fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money.

In total, he had eight overlapping full-time public-sector jobs between 2020 and 2023. During that time, this relatively junior civil servant was paid Â£350,000.

Soon after her call with Chowdhury, Adams discovered that he had logged into the call using a Defra email address. When she challenged him about it, he blamed it on a technical error. She told the jury at his trial at Southwark Crown Court: "I believed he was lying." She escalated her concerns up the chain.

But his fraudulent behaviour remained undetected. Chowdhury was able to keep working multiple government jobs for several more months.

It was the government's National Fraud Initiative that flagged suspected fraud to Defra. As a result, the Defra Investigations Team launched an investigation and Chowdhury was arrested and interviewed under caution in July 2023. He was dismissed by the civil service in September 2023.

On Monday he was found guilty of nine counts of fraud and told by the judge that he would be facing a "very substantial custodial sentence".

Chowdhury, 56, arrived each day at court alone. He is married, yet there never seemed to be any friends or family there to support him.

He would often make his way down the corridors of the court building carrying two plastic carrier bags, with a bobble hat perched on his head.

He sometimes looked bewildered in the witness box.

Chowdhury, who had a diagnosis of PTSD in 2015, said that he had taken on so many roles because he feared losing his livelihood after experiencing insecure employment. He said he'd been forced out of a job at the Department for Education in 2013.

He said he hadn't been thinking clearly when applying for jobs ''because of my stress, distress and trauma and due to my panic at the time".

But the prosecution argued his behaviour was "deliberate" and "intentional dishonesty" and that he had exploited the system.

Chowdhury sometimes looked bewildered in the witness box

However, the case raises important questions for the Civil Service. How was he able to get away with it for so long?

Part of the explanation is that the Civil Service's human resources system is not centralised. Chowdhury did not declare on government application forms that he was already a civil servant, so the system did not spot the duplication.

Hannah Keenan, who works at the Institute for Government, says the system clearly failed. "Government departments are taking hiring decisions independently of each other and without systems that join up," she said.

"That the model and the culture surrounding it aren't working shouldn't be a surprise though - we've known for a long time about poor performers flying under the radar by shuffling between jobs."

There are more than 550,000 people employed across the Civil Service, many in offices in Whitehall, in central London. They are responsible for carrying out government policy and the daily administration of the country.

Another reason why Chowdhury's fraud went undetected is that he was working entirely from the east London house he shared with his mother. In 2023, she had dementia and terminal cancer, and he told his employers that he needed to be at home to look after her.

But rather than reducing his workload to concentrate on her care, he took on more jobs. He covered up his impossible daily schedule by getting sick notes from his GP.

In April 2023, he was off with what he called "mixed anxiety and depressive disorder" from Defra, the UKHSA, the Department for Business and Trade and the Food Standards Agency.

While the sick notes were shown to the jury, they were not given evidence about what caused the depressive disorder or of a psychiatric assessment having been done during this period.

His sickness did not prevent him from starting yet another job â this time at the Home Office. He did not mention to them he was ill, nor did he tell them he was working elsewhere.

Unsurprisingly, a succession of his line managers told the jury he had not been very good at his jobs.

Especially damning was the evidence given by Dan McCartney, who managed Chowdhury at Defra. He said he had to "effectively tell him what to do the whole time" and that Chowdhury kept his camera off during video calls for four months, claiming technical problems.

But because Chowdhury was not subject to any formal performance measures at Defra, he was given a permanent job. He remained there for two years.

In September 2023, Chowdhury was found to have more than Â£40,000 in his bank accounts as well as significant investments, including Â£45,000 in premium bonds.

A recent survey of 1,700 civil servants by the independent think tank Re:State found 76% of respondents thought it did not do a good job of tackling poor performers.

An ex-senior civil servant told the BBC that much of the Civil Service is culturally averse to performance management.

"Whitehall's known for ages it has to be less risk averse. Shrinking the Civil Service and the adoption of artificial intelligence technology make that more vital," they said.

"But don't underestimate the fear factor: fear of drawn-out grievances and fear of being accused of wasting taxpayers' money if the Civil Service was to do what big private sector firms do, which is cut to get rid of poor performers by offering some money and a settlement agreement."

A Defra spokesperson said: "Kashim Chowdhury abused the trust placed in him as a public servant and defrauded taxpayers out of almost Â£285,000. He will now rightly face the consequences for his actions.

"This conviction is the result of a thorough and painstaking investigation led by the Defra Counter Fraud and Investigations team. It sends a clear message that fraud against the public purse will not be tolerated and those responsible will be held to account."

The spokesperson said Defra would use "all available avenues to recover the proceeds of Chowdhury's offending on behalf of taxpayers".

Chowdhury's case also illustrates a wider issue.

He was engaged in what is being called "polygamous working", where people hold down several jobs secretly. Websites have even been set up offering people advice on how to keep their employers in the dark.

Its rise has been blamed on larger numbers of people working from home.

The government's National Fraud Initiative has identified 23 cases of "polygamous working" across London in its most recent report. It uncovered Â£500,000 of overpaid salaries over a period of a few months.

Alongside the fraud case, Chowdhury is involved in a legal dispute with his three siblings, who accuse him of ''bullying'' their mother into signing over half of her Â£2.1m home to him in the final days of her life. He denies it.

At times during his criminal trial, Chowdhury appeared confused by what he was hearing and blamed trauma for the web of lies he had spun. The jury took seven hours to reach their verdict and decided he was motivated by personal gain - stealing tens of thousands of pounds from the taxpayer.

Chowdhury now faces a significant custodial sentence.

Civil servant who secretly held five jobs found guilty of fraud

#143. The superpower battle over energy – and what it means for your bills

The superpower battle over energy â and what it means for your bills

A gas rig in the North Sea. An oil field in Texas. A solar panel factory in China. They can feel a world away, but your energy bill says otherwise.

In January, it could jump by more than a quarter â around Â£460 extra a year for the typical UK household â according to the latest forecasts from the big suppliers British Gas, EDF and E.ON.

For the second time in four years, a war thousands of miles away is landing in British inboxes.

Since US and Israeli strikes on Iran in February effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz, oil and gas prices have soared.

Winter is expected to push them higher still: demand for gas is rising just as Europe's winter gas stores are at their lowest since records began in 2011.

And there's a bigger threat looming over all of this: the warming climate. Cutting carbon emissions is essential to limit future warming and avoid the most damaging effects of climate change. But keeping bills down while cutting emissions is proving hard â and not just for the UK.

As world leaders gather this week in Antalya, Turkey, for COP31, the UN climate summit, the same tension is playing out around the world. Energy and climate policy has become the front line in a contest between the world's two energy superpowers, leaving everyone else squeezed in between.

America is betting on fossil-fuel molecules: pumping record volumes of oil and gas and pressing its allies to buy them. China is betting on electrons: flooding the world with the solar panels, batteries and electric cars that can replace those fuels.

Whether the bet on fossil fuels or renewables wins will shape not just what the world pays for energy, but how fast it can cut the emissions that are heating the planet.

The UK, which already buys nearly a quarter of its imported gas from America and much of its clean energy kit from China, is being pulled both ways. So which path will it take? Is there a middle way and what will it all mean for your bills?

On the Moray Firth near Inverness, a long spit of sand and dunes curls out into the sea at Whiteness Head. Behind it lies a yard where, for nearly 30 years, thousands of workers built the giant platforms that opened up North Sea oil.

Ming Yang, one of China's biggest wind turbine makers, wanted to bring it back to life with a Â£1.5bn factory and up to 1,500 jobs initially generated. In March, after US lawmakers had urged the UK to block it, ministers did just that, citing national security.

Panorama, Fire and Floods: Our Extreme Weather

Last month, they approved a new North Sea gas field, Jackdaw. No to Chinese wind turbines; yes to North Sea gas.

Few countries are more exposed to the energy shock than the UK. Gas heats most homes and, much of the time, sets the wholesale price of electricity. The last shock, after Russia's invasion of Ukraine, cost households an extra Â£63bn â around Â£2,200 each â according to the Energy and Climate Intelligence Unit and E3G, two climate and energy research organisations.

Wind turbines represent homegrown energy, but they come with potential problems

Less than half of the UK's gas now comes from its own North Sea fields, and the energy we import often comes from places that turn out to be unstable or unreliable.

At the same time, Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh says tackling climate change remains a priority after Britain's hottest summer on record. The heatwaves, droughts and wildfires it brought would have been almost impossible without global warming, the Met Office's chief scientist told BBC Panorama.

But when I pressed Fahnbulleh on which matters most, bills or climate, she was unequivocal: "With the cost of living biting, affordability is my overriding objective, without a doubt."

"We are far too exposed to global markets we have no control over," Fahnbulleh told me. "What's the solution to that? Homegrown clean energy."

There are two ways to make Britain's energy more homegrown: drill for more of it, or build more of it.

Within hours of Jackdaw's approval, US President Donald Trump - at war with wind turbines since objecting to a wind farm near his Aberdeenshire golf course in 2006 - took to Truth Social. "They should go FULL BLAST!!!" he wrote. "TAKE DOWN THE COSTLY AND RIDICULOUS WINDMILLS!"

Now the UK government faces a tougher choice - whether to approve Rosebank, west of Shetland, the UK's largest undeveloped oil field.

Rosebank is far bigger than Jackdaw - Fahnbulleh has said it would produce about seven times the emissions - and it is mostly oil, not gas. The subject has reportedly split the cabinet. A decision is expected within weeks.

Fahnbulleh, centre, at Jackdaw last month. She says the drilling will have minimal impact on energy prices

The government's opponents are already blaming high bills on its reluctance to drill. Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch has told the prime minister to "stop faffing about" and give permission, calling drilling "the only way we can protect families from rising bills".

"If we use our own gasâ¦ that will bring our bills down," says Reform UK's Richard Tice.

Last week's US midterms were a reminder of the political risks. Soaring petrol prices are widely seen as a key reason Trump's Republicans lost control of Congress.

But when I asked Fahnbulleh whether producing more British oil and gas would bring bills down, she was clear. "No, it doesn't," she said. "In the end, the oil and gas is sold on global markets."

Critics go further. Rosebank "doesn't really do anything for energy security", argues Tessa Khan of the campaign group Uplift, which has fought the field in the courts. "What it does do is send a very dangerous signal about the UK's seriousness about addressing climate change after the summer that we've just had."

Zoom out, though, and the row looks rather different. The North Sea is in long-term decline. Output has fallen by more than two-thirds since its peak in 1999, and the Office for Budget Responsibility expects it to keep falling by around 10% a year â even with Rosebank, which its forecast assumes goes ahead.

Whatever ministers decide, the basin's most productive days are behind it. Jackdaw's reserves amount to only a few months of UK gas demand, while Rosebank contains less than a year's worth of our oil consumption.

The debate around Rosebank and Jackdaw has sparked vehement opposition from climate protesters

And last week the government introduced a bill to write its ban on new exploration licences into law. Rosebank, which is already licensed, is not covered. That could let ministers have it both ways: approve the last big field, while promising there will never be another.

With bills set to rise again in January, that may prove a tempting combination.

Gas is bought, burned and bought again. A wind turbine or solar panel costs money to put up but can then generate electricity for decades without needing shipments of fuel.

The government's climate advisers, the Climate Change Committee, calculate that a future price shock like 2022's would push household energy bills up 59% if Britain stayed on a high-carbon path - but only 4% if it focused on renewables.

The cost of power from new solar farms fell by 90% between 2010 and 2024, and from onshore wind by 70%, according to the International Renewable Energy Agency. But the recent bout of global inflation has been pushing initial costs back up. Last year the Danish developer Ãrsted scrapped plans for Hornsea 4, a giant wind farm off the Yorkshire coast, blaming soaring supply chain costs.

Higher interest rates have made things worse. Most of the cost of a wind or solar farm comes up front, so developers must borrow heavily before they generate any power.

And there is another problem. Britain needs expensive new electricity networks to bring that renewable power to the towns and cities where it is needed. Meanwhile, gas-fired stations still frequently set wholesale power prices - meaning any cost savings are not always reflected in customer bills.

All of which means building more renewables does not automatically mean lower household bills.

And there is another issue - China's dominance of clean tech. It makes the vast majority of the world's solar panels and batteries, and around two-thirds of its electric cars.

And China's lead is growing. In March, its exports of solar panels, batteries and electric cars hit a record, up 70% on a year earlier, according to the think tank Ember.

The UK wants to escape the fossil fuels that leave households exposed to price shocks and keep adding to global warming. Much of the technology needed to replace those fuels comes from China - a country it is increasingly reluctant to depend on.

I asked Fahnbulleh whether Britain was swapping dependence on petrostates for dependence on Beijing. "Well, look, we are," she said, adding: "we've got a diversified global market".

Britain will keep buying from China where it needs to, says Fahnbulleh, but she wants more of the kit made here. "I want to see turbines," she said.

Yet UK-built turbines were exactly what Ming Yang was offering.

"There was a security risk, and national security comes first and foremost," says Fahnbulleh. The government has never publicly explained what that risk was.

Two days before Jackdaw was approved, Japan's Sumitomo Electric opened Britain's first HVDC subsea cable factory at Nigg, a short way up the coast from Whiteness Head, creating up to 250 jobs.

Foreign investment, then - just not from China.

Earlier this year the US formally left the Paris climate agreement and is not expected to send a delegation to this year's COP. Without American pressure, others have less reason to make bold promises: national pledges now add up to a cut in global emissions of just 6% by 2035, the UN says â a tenth of what scientists say is needed to limit warming to 1.5C.

Donald Trump is a big exponent of fossil fuels

China is beginning to fill the space. Li Shuo, of the Asia Society Policy Institute, calls it a "role reversal". Once "a very conservative player" at climate talks, China now sees pushing the global climate agenda as serving its national interest â and, he says, "we're only at the beginning of this". It aims to hit one of COP31's flagship goals, getting 35% of its energy from electricity, five years early.

And increasingly it is Chinese companies, rather than governments' promises, that are driving the transition. "It's really just a China story," says Li Shuo.

If that is right, it leaves the UK in an uncomfortable place. Hard choices â and they come when household budgets are already stretched thin.

Thatcher's Right to Buy created a generation of homeowners - did it also fuel the housing crisis?

The NHS is relying more on charities - and that might be creating problems for patients

Democrats have an identity crisis - it won't be an easy fix
