#141. Prediction markets tied to clinical trials and drug approvals draw lawmakers' ire

Prediction market event contracts on both clinical drug trials and Food and Drug Administration regulatory approvals are drawing attention from two Democratic senators, according to a letter obtained first by CNBC.

Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, D-Md., and Sen. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., expressed concern over the related event contracts in a Wednesday letter to Department of Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. The senators asked what measures HHS has taken to mitigate any risks that may arise from the markets.

"Allowing individuals to bet on the success of these trials and drugs undermines the scientific process, creates perverse incentives for researchers, harms trial participants, and could lead to dangerous medical outcomes for untold numbers of Americans," the senators wrote.

Alsobrooks and Wyden repeated concerns raised by some healthcare professionals that the markets could alter patient thinking when considering whether to participate in a trial and expressed concern over potential insider trading risks from researchers and regulators who may have access to nonpublic information.

The senators requested HHS provide all internal communication regarding staff participation in prediction markets tied to matters governed by the department. They also requested all communication between the department and the prediction market platforms themselves, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — which regulates event contracts — and any internal analyses that have been done regarding the potential impact of the markets.

"HHS, FDA, and [the National Institutes of Health] must uphold the integrity of the drug development process and maintain public trust in the public health by putting essential guardrails in place to prevent individuals profiting on the success and approval of drugs that the American people depend on," Alsobrooks and Wyden said in the letter.

Both leading prediction market platforms, Kalshi and Polymarket, offer the contracts, although in Polymarket's case the markets can only be found on its international exchange, which isn't regulated in the U.S.

When Kalshi launched its related markets in July, the company said it put in place the guardrails, such as only allowing markets related to late-stage trials, in order to limit the risk of trading. Kalshi also vowed to list contracts only after a trial had completed enrollment and to use employee verification technology for all traders participating in the markets.

"Drug development is one of the most important and most information-constrained industries on earth," Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour said in July. "The data that determines which drugs advance and which don't is largely locked away from the people who need it most. Surfacing information is what Kalshi is for, and we are committed to doing it right."

Kalshi noted that investors also buy shares in companies in fields including biotechnology and pharmaceuticals that are awaiting regulatory approvals.

"The hypothetical concerns of the Senators apply equally to the equities market, which appears to be left out of their letter.," Kalshi spokesperson Jack Such told CNBC. "The only difference is that the size — and incentive for bad activity — is significantly higher in the equities market than it is on Kalshi."

Polymarket and the Department of Health and Human Services didn't immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#139. Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

US President Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the chief executive of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump said he spoke to Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Speaking later at a White House press briefing, Trump told reporters, "I know the FBI is looking into" the affair but did not give further details on the investigation, or confirm when it was launched.

Trump added he thought that Huang was an "honourable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

The FBI declined to comment when contacted by the BBC.

Nvidia, which has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company after Apple, and designs computer chips that have become central to AI technology.

The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off Nvidia's value on Monday as the company's share price fell by more than 4%.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report. She is not related to Jensen Huang.

According to the newspaper, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington in spring this year, warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Nvidia's Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

Megaspeed's office in Singapore's central business district appeared to be operational and staffed on Tuesday.

The BBC was refused entry to its floor, but spoke to a staff member by phone who said they had no comment and directed all queries to the company's PR team.

The BBC has emailed Megaspeed several times for comment.

Nvidia, co-founded by Jensen Huang, is also a major focus in the AI technology race between the US and China, with officials in Washington treating its exports to Beijing as a matter of national security - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked concerns.

Also on Monday, Huang and Nvidia were sued in a proposed class action lawsuit in California that accuses them of defrauding shareholders by concealing his ties with the suspected Chinese spy.

The lawsuit alleges that the company lied to the media about Jensen Huang's relationship with Alice Huang, "who is suspected of seeking to help smuggle Nvidia products to China."

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment on the lawsuit.

At the White House, Trump was asked several times when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president added.

He said he spoke to Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Huang has admitted that he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week, however it is unclear whether Huang will appear.

A letter said the briefing would be "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Some workers the BBC spoke to at Nvidia's Santa Clara headquarters on Monday said it was business as usual, despite the intense attention on the company.

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI buildout that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Nvidia's most advanced AI semiconductors including the GB200 Blackwell super chip remain blocked from the Chinese market.

Additional reporting by Tessa Wong in Singapore

#136. Faisal Islam: What the boss of US tech giant Nvidia told me about China and national security

Watch: Jensen Huang speaks to the BBC's Faisal Islam in 2025

Jensen Huang has the air of the late Steve Jobs about him. I have interviewed both, and the up-close charisma - and occasionally scratchy genius - is shared.

Both sported a signature item of black clothing, although Huang opts for a leather jacket rather than the Apple founder's turtle neck.

Both are innovators revered in the tech world. However, Huang is now being investigated over an affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Everything you have heard about Artificial Intelligence (AI) over the past few months - good, bad, revolutionary and catastrophic - is dependent on having the most powerful frontier AI models.

In turn they depend on the most powerful microchips, and for years that has been this very company, Nvidia, led by Huang, and his technology rooted in repurposed games graphics and the manufacturing prowess of Taiwan.

Names such as Gracehopper, Vera and Rubin mean almost nothing to the public, but are the names of chips powering the creation of AI models transforming the world.

I met Huang as he held court with reporters and offered words of encouragement for the UK's tech future during US President Donald Trump's state visit to the UK in September last year.

It was also right in the middle of his attempts to get Trump to lift export bans to China of some of the highest performing AI chips, specifically the H200.

An export ban on a less powerful chip had already been lifted. He was, I suggested to him, one of the few people who could walk into the White House to meet Trump, and a few days later meet President Xi Jinping in Beijing.

He made no secret of his views, nor his assessment that he had persuaded Trump of his case.

"President Trump is very clear. He wants America to win and and President Xi wants China to win, and it's possible for both of them too. And the reason for that is because it's not a zero-sum game. The advance of human society is not a zero-sum game."

He is probably the only person in the world that could have spoken that sentence with the authority that he might have heard personally from both these superpower leaders.

However, his view about "zero-sum game" is not backed by many in national security circles.

Their view is that the race to the most advanced forms of AI is entirely zero sum, and requires the West - and the US in particular - to take critical action to hold back Chinese advancement.

The country that creates the most powerful AI systems, from the most powerful chips, will win, in terms of scientific development, of cracking encryption, economic growth and cyber-weapons.

He made a further argument: Far from being a national security challenge, it was, in fact, better for the US that the world "including China" became dependent on US systems, what they called the "American tech stack", with Nvidia chips at its core.

Huang then went even further than that, saying that President Trump "is very clear-eyed" about the idea that "this computing technology is not a national security concern".

He told me "any military is smart enough to build their military on top of their own indigenous technology, and not rely on an adversary to build their technology, build their military capability".

At that time, which is the same moment now being independently investigated over national security concerns and alleged Chinese espionage, Huang was making clear public arguments to lift export bans, and seemed to have a clear idea of President Trump's views.

More than that, he was also disputing the idea that his technology, in China's hands, was any type of national security concern.

Nvidia is the absolute epicentre of the AI tech boom in the US and of the global race for AI supremacy.

Even if the boom turned to bust, Nvidia is still expected to remain in rude health.

It has actual hard revenues of about $400bn this year. Monday's drop of about 4% of its value reflects new and extraordinary risks at this junction of tech and global politics, but the chipmaker is still worth more than $4 trillion.

#134. I took Vinted to court to get my money back

Mali Osmalek says she was stuck in a cycle of automated responses from Vinted

Mali Osmelak never imagined that selling clothes on Vinted would end with a court claim to recover almost Â£6,000.

She started trading on the platform to earn an income while managing Crohn's disease and caring for her four-year-old daughter.

But when she became a professional seller in March, she was unable to complete Vinted's verification process, leaving thousands of pounds inaccessible in her Vinted wallet. After several weeks of trying to resolve the issue, Mali took legal action.

Vinted says sellers may be asked to complete verification checks to comply with legal and regulatory requirements and 98% of transactions are completed smoothly without needing help from its support team.

Vinted is hugely popular in the UK with 17 million users. The online marketplace does not charge a fee to sellers for listing on there.

After the BBC reported on users whose accounts had been suspended or banned, dozens of people got in touch describing difficulties accessing money held in their Vinted wallets.

Mali joined Vinted in 2024 as a side-hustle. Demand for her second-hand gymwear grew rapidly and, within months, she had set up a limited company to expand the business.

This year she signed up for a Vinted Pro seller account - designed for people who want to sell a high volume of items and who are registered as a business.

Within weeks she had generated almost Â£6,000 in sales. But when she tried to withdraw the money, she discovered her account had been restricted and was told she needed to complete a verification process.

"I thought, 'That's OK. It's not rocket science,'" she says. "I'd set up the business with the help of an accountant and had all the information they needed."

Mali says she spotted an error Vinted had made with her name and she flagged it with Vinted, but "again nothing was done and the verification kept failing". She found herself stuck in a cycle of automated responses.

In an effort to break the deadlock, she also contacted Mangopay, the payments company that at the time operated Vinted sellers' wallets and processed transactions on the platform. She was directed back to Vinted.

After eight weeks without access to the money, Mali decided to take both companies to the small claims court, spending Â£455 in court fees.

Three days after the court claim was filed, Mali says the account was "magically verified" and she could withdraw the funds.

She still pressed ahead with the case though to try to recover her court fees, costs and interest.

In June, she obtained a default judgement after neither Vinted nor Mangopay responded to her claim, with the court ordering the companies to pay Â£1,207.50.

But she has yet to receive the money. She has since been informed that Vinted and Mangopay have applied to have the judgement set aside, with a hearing scheduled for December.

"It's been ridiculous and incredibly stressful," she says.

"I need that cash. I will fight it. I'm going to tell the court their system is completely broken. I needed help and nobody helped me."

Vinted said: "Ms Osmelak's check was not successful because her documents did not match the name on her Vinted account. We have since helped her update it, and her account is now verified."

The company did not respond to the BBC's questions about the legal action Mali has taken and continues to pursue.

Mangopay said it was "bound to professional secrecy and data protection obligations. We are, therefore, not allowed to comment on individual cases involving our clients or their users, nor can we share details about specific accounts, transactions or legal matters."

Azri Mukthar says he has now stopped selling on Vinted

Sportswear seller Azri Mukthar told the BBC he has been trying to withdraw almost Â£2,500 from his account for over a year.

The businessman was more than happy to become a Pro seller as he was already registered as a limited company.

"I submitted all the documentation they requested and got the go-ahead from Vinted. So then I uploaded all the products and got 30 to 40 sales immediately," he says.

But when he wanted to withdraw his earnings, he found that he couldn't.

"They said my verification was pending," he says.

Azri shared a frustrated conversation he had with Vinted's AI assistant

Azri too, says he's been round in endless circles repeatedly submitting documents only to get an immediate response saying they had been rejected.

"It's frustrating and annoying as there's no clear system to follow.

"You contact customer support and they say you've not submitted X, Y or Z. But I know I have done all they're asking for."

He claims a certificate from Companies House that Vinted requested has been declined 15 times.

Azri has now stopped selling on Vinted and says he is going to have to start legal action.

Vinted said: "Mr Mukthar's check could not be completed because he did not provide all the required documents. Our support team is currently in touch with him to help with his verification."

Have you had a similar experience when becoming a professional seller? Share your story.

#133. CoreWeave soars more than 24% for best day since April 2025 after strong Q3 report

CoreWeave shares jumped 24.71% Wednesday for its best day since April 2025 after the neocloud posted quarterly earnings that beat estimates and raised its guidance for the year.

"Demand for the infrastructure is and remains overwhelming." CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Wednesday.

Neoclouds such as CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN have come under scrutiny this month as investors assessed the sustainability of the AI trade. CoreWeave, in particular, sparked alarm when spreads on its CDS widened past 11%, generally viewed as a distress level, as investors have to pay up for protection against default. With the price of its high-yield debt climbing, CoreWeave highlighted additional routes to finance its data center build-out with less alarming yields, quelling investor concerns for the time being.

"Our asset level debt has basically been rock solid through this entire thing," Intrator told CNBC. "There is an enormous demand for that product. We're about to launch another asset-level debt in the next week or two that has already been rated high investment grade." Intrator added that he expects it "will be underwritten in the market at 7% to 7.5% interest," which will bring down the average weighted cost of capital for the company.

The three months to Sept. 30 marked a "breakout quarter" for CoreWeave, Intrator said on the conference call, adding that growth was supported by multiple sources of funding, including financing backed by customer contracts and around $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits.

Revenue jumped 168% to $3.7 billion in the third quarter, the company said in a Tuesday statement.

Other neclouds also rose on CoreWeave's strong results, with Nebius rising 15.44%, Applied Digital 7.37% and Iren 5.63% Wednesday.

Despite fears that a "blue wave" in last week's midterm elections could restrict data center capacity, Intrator reiterated that data center growth looks poised to continue as expected for CoreWeave.

"We don't see any impact on our ability to deliver the data centers within the U.S. that are required for us to deliver on our backlog," Intrator told CNBC on Wednesday. "In Texas, Governor-elect [Gina Hinojosa], said that facilities that are permitted and under construction will continue, and we've been really, really thoughtful about the broad distribution."

CoreWeave expects $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

The neocloud has done well to allay concerns about financing by moving away from unsecured bond financing, Zacks Chief Equity Strategist John Blank told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

"It might be the bottom for CoreWeave," Blank said Wednesday. "But the shares are so beaten up, you have to believe at this point there's more value on the upside than the down."

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Caleigh Keating contributed to this report.

#130. US inflation higher than expected in October - here's what is happening with prices

US inflation rose at a faster-than-expected pace in October, according to official figures.

Prices rose by 0.4% in the month, which was lower than the 0.6% increase in September.

However, analysts had expected prices to rise by 0.3%, and the higher-than-expected pace could add to arguments for the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates at its next meeting in December.

The new figures come a week after the midterm elections which were fought over cost-of-living pressures across the US.

Inflation is the rate at which the price of products or services increases over time.

For example, if a bottle of milk costs $1 but rises to $1.10 a year later, then annual inflation for bottled milk is 10%.

Officials from the Bureau of Labor Statistics measure inflation through the consumer price index (CPI).

It tracks the price changes of hundreds of day-to-day items including fresh food, gasoline and medical services to work out the overall level of inflation in the US economy.

The bureau updates this so-called basket of goods over time as peoples' spending and lifestyle habits change.

There are several factors that can cause inflation to rise.

If demand for a product outstrips its supply, that can lead to producers raising prices - the craze for Labubu dolls in recent years and the high resale prices are an example.

Higher incomes can also lead to higher demand, for example, which in turn pushes up prices.

Supply and demand: Labubu dolls illustrate how inflation can rise

Supply can also affect inflation. If supply of a product is reduced, but demand remains constant, that can cause prices to rise. A good example of this is crude oil, which is used to make gasoline and diesel.

Before the US-Israel war with Iran began, it was trading at just over $70 a barrel, but when the conflict constricted crude supplies, the price quickly rose and is currently over $100.

Over the month of October, prices rose by 0.4%, driven by increases in housing, food and energy costs.

However, the pace of energy price rises, at 0.7% in October, was much lower than the 5.2% increase seen in September.

While the Bureau of Labor Statistics would normally provide figures for the 12-month change in prices, it did not collect data in October 2025 so it could not provide that estimate.

For the 12 months to September, inflation was 3.7%.

The US Federal Reserve has a target for keeping annual inflation at 2%.

When inflation is too high the Fed tends to raise interest rates in order to encourage people and businesses to save more and spend less money in the hope this will slow price rises.

While there was no annual figure published for inflation this month, analysts say the fact inflation was higher in October than expected increases the chance of an interest rate rise at the Fed's December meeting.

Federal Reserve governor Chirstopher Waller, who spoke before the latest inflation figures were published, also suggested he was leaning towards a rate increase in December even if October's figures were slower than expected.

US interest rates held steady and economy grows - what it means for you

Since coming into office for the second time, President Donald Trump has introduced tariffs on most US trading partners around the world.

While most of the additional global levies were outlawed by the Supreme Court in February, the president immediately announced a new 10% global tariff to replace them.

Trump argued the tariffs would increase America's tax earnings, boost manufacturing and encourage people to buy US-made goods, but the higher import taxes have also had an impact on inflation, according to research from the Federal Reserve Bank of New York.

Higher tariffs - which are levies that businesses pay on the goods they import - directly raise prices for US companies unless the overseas firms they are buying from lower prices to absorb some of the cost.

In response to the new tariffs, the New York Fed found exporters reduced prices very little, meaning US-based businesses ended up absorbing nearly 90% of the cost of the added tax.

Indirectly, higher tariffs also affected the prices of locally made goods, as many US producers rely on parts or materials from overseas.

Overall, the Fed found that every percentage point increase in average tariffs led to nearly a 0.25% price increase for consumer goods after one year.

While inflation is currently above where the Federal Reserve would like it has been much higher in previous years.

During the Covid pandemic, issues such as supply chain disruption started pushing inflation up.

Additionally, in early 2022 when Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine, oil prices surged over $100 a barrel.

Both of these factors helped to drive inflation to a peak of about 9%.

It's important to note that if inflation is rising at a slower rate, that does not mean prices are going down - it simply means prices are rising at a slower pace.

A bit of inflation is considered good for an economy as the Federal Reserve's target rate illustrates.

Many central banks consider inflation of about 2% or a little higher to be stable, and at a level that doesn't impact people's purchasing decisions.

Much below that, people can delay spending if they expect price rises to slow further, or to fall. That can lead to businesses reducing wages, or reducing the number of workers to cope with lower demand.

The value of social security benefits is changed to largely keep up with inflation, protecting people's ability to purchase their usual goods and services through the cost-of-living adjustment.

Shortly after September's inflation figure was released, the Social Security Administration announced that Social Security benefits including old age, survivors and disability insurance would rise by a similar amount - 3.6% - in 2027.

The administration said that the 3.6% rise will mean an average retirement benefit increase of $75 a month.

The cost-of-living adjustment will take effect in January for about 72 million people, while increased payments for the 7.2m receiving the supplemental security income will start on 31 December.

'It's rough out here': Americans struggle with rising inflation

Which war is having the biggest impact on diesel prices - Iran or Ukraine?

Trump wants to reduce the cost of fuel as the midterms loom - will it work?

#129. Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

#127. Kim Kardashian's Skims offers refunds for $148 advent calendar after backlash

Kim Kardashian's firm Skims will refund people who bought the advent calendar

Kim Kardashian's Skims is offering customers refunds for its $148 (Â£110) advent calendar which has been widely criticised on social media.

The accessories advent calendar promised "twelve days of fun surprises" but has been mocked online for the quality of its contents, such as hair clips and keychains, with some comparing them to something you would find down the back of a sofa.

A spokesperson for Skims has told a number of media outlets "for some, the calendar didn't meet expectations" and the company has "personally contacted every customer" to offer a full refund or credit to put towards other products".

The shapewear and clothing business was co-founded by Kardashian in 2019 and has made the reality TV star a billionaire, according to Forbes magazine.

Skims was valued at $5bn in 2025 when it raised millions of dollars in new funding.

One account on TikTok called spicychathouse compared the contents of the calendar to things "my cat collects from around my house". Another account said it looked like "a bunch of stuff that Kim Kardashian was emptying out of her bedside table".

A number of accounts showed people cleaning out their own sofas and claiming they had accidentally created the Skims advent calendar.

"It is really interesting to see this kind of backlash," said Kate Vlietstra, senior director of food and drink at market research analyst Mintel.

"It is something we have been seeing a bit more in recent years, particularly with the trend for unboxing on social media," she said,

"It means that, inevitably, if people are not feeling they're getting the value that they have hoped for from the advent calendar...they've purchased, then yes, there is going to be a backlash."

Advent calendars remain popular, according to Helen Wing, head of innovation at market research company Ipsos though she said that "the buzz around them has reduced a little bit".

She said according to Ipsos research, people are willing to pay a bit more for an advent calendar but the contents have to justify the price tag.

Using a Â£285 advent calendar sold by Liberty as an example, Wing said the upmarket department story is "very open about the value of the contents".

"They will say there is over Â£1,000 worth of content," she said. "If you've got that reassurance when you're making the purchase, particularly if it is for a gift, then it is important to have some faith that the contents are actually going to be worth the money that you're paying."

#125. Mississippi Senate: Scott Colom lead over Hyde-Smith pops to more than 4,400 votes

Democratic nominee Scott Colom on Wednesday significantly expanded his lead over incumbent Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate election to more than 4,400 votes as the ballot count continued.

When the day began, Colom had a 2,990-vote lead over Hyde-Smith.

That spread ticked upward slowly until just after 2 p.m. ET when a surge of newly counted ballots boosted his tally by 607 votes, and then soon after by another 621 votes.

As of 2:30 p.m., Colom was ahead of Hyde-Smith by 4,404 votes, according to the Associated Press.

With an estimated 99% of the votes in, Colom led Hyde-Smith by 49.18% to 48.72%. Independent candidate Ty Pinkins had 2.1% of the vote.

Colom has steadily expanded his lead over Hyde-Smith for days as he has won a higher share of so-called affidavit ballots than he received in ballots counted on Election Day night. Affidavit ballots are cast by voters on a provisional basis if there is a question about the voter's eligibility.

The AP estimates that fewer than 15,000 ballots are left to be processed.

Colom was boosted by newly counted votes from Hinds County, the second-most populous county in Mississippi, which encompasses the capital of Jackson.

Most of the county's residents are, like Colom, Black.

If Colom is elected, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the Reconstruction Era after the Civil War.

In 2024, Democratic presidential nominee Kamala Harris defeated President Donald Trump by 46 percentage points in Hinds County. As of Wednesday morning, Colom was winning 80% of the vote in the county, with just 19% for Hyde-Smith.

Mississippi, which has not elected a Democratic senator since the segregationist John Stennis in 1982, was on the radar of few, if any, political analysts as a possible pickup for Democrats.

Tuesday was the deadline for resolving affidavit ballots. It is also the deadline for the receipt of mail-in absentee ballots that are postmarked by Election Day, Nov. 3.

Mississippi is one of just two states whose Senate election has not been called. Alaska is the other.

Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats held by Republicans going into the election. That gives Democrats a new majority in the Senate of at least 54 seats in the 100-member chamber come January.

Alaska before Election Day was viewed as possibly key to the question of whether Republicans would retain their narrow majority in the Senate.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola's lead extended to 4,250 votes over incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan, with 84% of the total votes counted, a margin of 1.8 percentage points.

If neither she nor Sullivan gets more than 50% of the total vote, the race will go to a ranked-choice round, in which the two other candidates, Gerald Heikes and another candidate named Dan Sullivan, will be eliminated, and the second choices of voters who made them their first choice will be allocated between Peltola and the incumbent.

#124. Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Sales of essential goods such as bottled water and canned food soared in the 10 days following the launch of the government's "Be Ready" emergency preparedness campaign on 22 October, research indicates.

Hand sanitiser sales were up 83%, sales of bottled water rose 67% and tinned and jarred goods climbed 37%, according to data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator.

The government's public awareness campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

However, Worldpanel said that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days-worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

After the campaign launch, the equivalent of an extra 400,000 people per day bought tinned meat, Worldpanel said, although the typical amount remained at 500g, which suggests a gradual stocking-up.

"Shoppers clearly took notice of the 'Be Ready' campaign, but this wasn't panic buying, with less than a third of households buying bottled water in October," said Fraser McKevitt, head of retail and consumer insight at Worldpanel by Numerator.

"Growth came from more people adding the recommended items on the government's checklist to their weekly shop, not from shoppers piling their trolleys high, which is very much in line with what the government wanted households to do," he said.

Sales of tinned and jarred goods rose from Â£58.2m in the 10 days to 2 November 2025 to Â£79.5m over the same period in 2026.

Bottled water sales jumped from Â£13.6m to Â£22.8m compared with a year earlier and hand sanitiser sales climbed from Â£497,000 to Â£908,000.

Further evidence of stockpiling came from food firm Princes on Tuesday, which said demand for tinned tuna, meat and vegetables had risen following the government's campaign.

Princes interim chief executive Giuseppe Mastrolia said the jump in demand "further reinforces the relevance of our core categories in providing affordable, nutritious and convenient food solutions".

The "Be Ready" campaign warns that severe weather and cyber attacks could disrupt essential services, and British households need to make sure they have what they need - for example, bottled water, and long-life food that doesn't need to be kept in the fridge such as dried fruit and nuts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said everyone should have "those supplies in the house to get you through those moments".

Disaster recovery expert Lucy Easthope previously told the BBC that the UK was playing "massive catch up" to other European nations in terms of preparedness.

These include France, Germany Denmark, Sweden, and Norway.

A number of experts have been calling for the UK to put more emphasis on food security, with some arguing that the government itself should take responsibility for food stockpiling, rather than individual households.

In October, a group of more than 180 experts signed an open letter, external to Burnham and first secretary of state Louise Haigh calling for local authorities to have a duty to prevent and respond to food supply emergencies.

They pointed out that a government survey found that 29% of people in the UK could not afford to keep a few days worth of food in the house.

Have you been stockpiling? If so, what have you been buying?

#122. Claude chatbot maker set for $1.3tn stock market debut despite 'catastrophic' AI warning

Anthropic, the artificial intelligence (AI) company behind the Claude chatbot, could be valued at around $1.3 trillion (Â£1 trillion) when it lists its shares for sale on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

The loss-making company says it will offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each and will raise up to $64bn when it floats this month.

It would value Anthropic at between $1.1tn and $1.3tn which, although large, is below the $2tn some backers of the firm predicted in summer, according to the Financial Times.

The huge valuation comes despite the company warning last month that AI could pose "catastrophic or existential risks to humanity".

It is floating at a volatile time for big tech firms. After last week's US midterm elections, the Nasdaq fell sharply on concerns a Democrat-led Congress could slow development of AI and the buildout of data centres.

There are also wider fears over the billions of dollars being spent on AI and whether it will pay off.

In its most recent results for the six months to the end of June, Anthropic's revenues jumped to $16.2bn from $1.1bn in the same period last year.

However, its pre-tax loss jumped to $80.7bn from $12.9bn.

Fiona Cincotta, a senior market analyst at City Index, said the $64bn Anthropic wants to raise is "fairly bullish" within "the volatility [and] concerns towards AI that we've seen in sort of particularly last week".

She told Reuters: "It's showing that they are still sort of upbeat about the outlook as far as AI is concerned and that obviously that they think that the demand is going to be there."

Anthropic, which will list under the symbol ANT on 19 November, will be the first fully AI-focused company to go public, making it a test case for the future of the technology.

At $1.3tn, Anthropic's possible valuation is below SpaceX, Elon Musk's rocket, satellite and artificial intelligence company, which listed its shares in June and was valued at nearly $1.8tn.

Global stocks plunge as fears over US AI slowdown spread to Asia and Europe

Anthropic's blockbuster share listing - 5 surprising things we now know

Anthropic is just five years old. It was co-founded in 2021 by brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei along with five others â all of whom worked at OpenAI, the rival AI giant behind ChatGPT.

It announced its intention to list on the stock market in June and OpenAI followed suit. That listing is now expected to occur early next year.

The listing announcements were followed by a series of incidents involving AI agents, which are bots designed to operate somewhat autonomously, going rogue.

Current and former staff of both companies have warned AI technology was being developed without the ability to protect humanity from possible future harms enacted by AI.

Anthropic has admitted that advanced AI systems "could, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity, particularly if such systems were misused, replicated, weaponised, or allowed to operate with a high degree of autonomy".

The company has reiterated promises to develop AI to be beneficial to people.

It has warned investors its future success depends largely on its ability to develop the most advanced AI models, and that "any failure to do so could adversely affect our business".

#120. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#119. From Paris to DC - why offices are being turned into apartments

Le Diamant office building is to close and be converted into apartments

When Roland Cubin walks you round what will eventually be a new Parisian apartment you have to use your imagination.

"Here you will be in the kitchen, and here is the bathroom," says the deputy CEO of French real estate company Groupama Immobilier.

It all sounds appealing, an upmarket flat in the west of Paris, but presently the space doesn't look like one at all. That's because it's currently a corner of one floor of a nine-storey office building called Le Diamant, where 1,800 people work.

Groupama Immobilier intends to convert the entire building into apartments. It should be the first of many such office-to-apartments conversions in the surrounding area.

We are in La DÃ©fense, the purpose-built, business district of the French capital, where the city has congregated its modern skyscrapers.

Mirroring a similar trend in places such as New York, Washington DC and London; Paris La DÃ©fense, the public development authority that runs the area, has unveiled plans to turn up to a fifth of the office buildings into housing.

Cubin leads us into what is currently a conference room in the centre of the building. It has no windows, which is indicative of a big challenge when turning offices into apartments â what to do with the large amount of space in the middle of every floor that doesn't get any natural light.

"There won't be any rooms there. Just access," says Roland Cubin, in reference to lifts and stairs.

Other issues include the need for more plumbing, and ceilings that can be too low.

Despite these challenges, there is a simple reason why there are plans for so many conversions â demand for offices in La DÃ©fense and across Paris has plummeted since the Covid-19 pandemic and the growth of homeworking.

Authorities want La DÃ©fense to become an area were people also live and play

"La DÃ©fense has a vacancy rate of 14-15% which is higher than it used to be," says Pierre-Yves Guice, CEO of Paris La DÃ©fense.

He adds that another reason for the conversion programme is the need to transform the business district from a place where the only thing people do is work.

"To retain companies in France and in Western Europe we need to diversify these office landscapes of the last century, because no-one wants to work in such an environment any longer," says Guice.

Ludovic Delaisse, CEO of the French arm of the Canadian real estate business Colliers, says that demand for office space in Paris has fallen by almost a third over the past five to six years.

He adds that office high rises will continue to be needed, but that cities need to make sure that leisure time can be enjoyed around them, such as having restaurants, bars, cinemas and gyms on their doorstep.

"People like the idea of grouping together in a centre of expertise [for work] - in tech hubs, for example. The challenge for La DÃ©fense is to be a place where you want to be, that you want to see when you leave work in the evening."

The Geneva building is the biggest office to apartments conversion in Washington DC

Inspiration for La DÃ©fense can come from Washington DC and New York City, where tax cuts for property developers are fuelling the largest amount of office to apartment conversions in the US. Like in Paris, this is in response to falling demand for office space since the pandemic.

A report last year, external found that 8,310 converted flats were in the pipeline in New York, and 6,533 in Washington.

In third place was Los Angeles on 4,322, followed by Chicago on 3,606.

"We set a goal of introducing 15,000 new residents downtown by 2028 and we are now on track to doing that," says Washington DC's deputy mayor for planning and development Nina Albert.

She says that while "there may be one or two pretty significant building demolitionsâ¦ the majority are office conversions".

As in Paris, they've faced technical challenges. They've sometimes solved the natural light problem in the middle of office buildings by creating atriums â light sinks in other words â in the middle of them.

Albert adds that about "hundreds of millions of dollars of private investment" has flooded into the sector.

In addition to tax cuts, the DC Department of Buildings, part of the Council of the District of Columbia, has eased planning restrictions to make it easier for office conversion schemes to gain official approval in the US capital. It's a similar case in New York City.

In the UK, there are also tax cuts and eased planning rules, external for converting offices into apartments. This has led to a continuing boom in conversions. A report last year by property firm Knight Frank said that the number of such projects in London were 60% higher , externalthan pre-Covid levels.

Back in Paris, Cubin hopes that French authorities follow these examples and reduce both taxes and rules regarding conversions.

"Just how the government deals with the tax question will decide whether France will be able to convert 2% of its vacant offices or 18 to 23%," he says.

"And at the moment, when you transform a building in a way that changes its function, it is classed not as an old building that's been changed but as a new building with all the regulatory demands that go with that regarding car parking spaces, wheelchair access, fire safety and so on."

As Christmas rush looms, how do China-made toys find their way to the US?

How oil-rich Alberta's economy might fare if it broke away from Canada

#117. Canada’s ambassador warns Trump tariffs are driving up costs, threatening U.S. investment

Canada's ambassador to the U.S. warned Wednesday that President Donald Trump's tariffs are driving up investment costs and discouraging business expansion, as new Federal Reserve research points to more price hikes for American consumers next year.

"Uncertainty increases your cost of capital," Ambassador Mark Wiseman said at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"If you're going to build a new plant, add a new ship, spend money on R&D, the more certainty you have, the more likely you're going to do that," said Wiseman, a former BlackRock executive who became Canada's ambassador in February.

His warning comes as Washington and Ottawa struggle to resolve a tariff quarrel affecting one of the U.S.'s largest trading relationships. The two countries exchanged roughly $570 billion in goods during the first nine months of 2026, according to U.S. Census Bureau data. That's an increase from the same period in 2025, the first year of Trump's second term, but lower than the comparable nine months during the previous three years, when Joe Biden was president.

U.S. customs duties calculated on imports from Canada reached $10 billion in 2025, compared with $400 million in 2024, according to the Congressional Research Service.

An October study by New York Fed researchers found that nearly 90% of tariff costs were passed on to U.S. importers, with about a quarter of tariff increases eventually reaching consumer goods prices and further price pressures expected in 2027.

The findings come as Labor Department data showed consumer prices rose a higher-than-expected 0.4% in October, adding to inflation concerns after Republicans' sweeping midterm losses.

Trump has defended his tariffs as necessary to protect U.S. manufacturers and workers, accusing Canada of discriminatory trade practices that disadvantage U.S. exporters.

The two governments have resumed negotiations after talks collapsed in August, with discussions focused on reducing tariffs on Canadian steel and automobiles. Trump has imposed duties of up to 50% on certain Canadian products, prompting retaliatory measures targeting billions of dollars in U.S. exports.

Wiseman called for the removal of tariffs imposed under Sections 232 and 338 of U.S. trade law, saying a resolution would help businesses make long-term investment decisions.

The political stakes have shifted following the midterms, with Democrats preparing to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January.

Still, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said Tuesday that the Trump administration remains Canada's principal negotiating partner.

"We've had comprehensive free trade with the United States since 1988," Wiseman said, describing Canada's objective as returning "as close as we can to free trade" while creating the certainty businesses need.

European ambassadors echoed those concerns at the B20 summit. Germany's ambassador to the U.S., Jens Hanefeld, said businesses could adapt to tariffs but struggled with an "ever-changing regulatory environment" that forced companies to spend more on lawyers to navigate shifting trade rules.

Speaking on the same panel, France's ambassador, Aurélien Lechevallier, urged greater cooperation on AI and energy rather than continued trade disputes.

"We've spent, I think, a lot of time the last years working and dealing with regulations, tariffs," he said. "Businesses are eager to do more business."

#116. Nvidia hires major US law firm to investigate alleged Chinese spy affair

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has become the face of the US AI boom

Nvidia has hired one of the largest law firms in the US to investigate the affair between its own chief executive and an alleged Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang, who co-founded the tech company, has admitted to having a romantic relationship outside of his marriage. The woman, Alice Huang, is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Nvidia said on Tuesday that it had hired New York law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, and that its attorney Joon Kim would lead "a thorough and independent investigation". The FBI is also conducting its own probe.

The company's share price has fallen almost to a yearly low, with $500bn (Â£375bn) wiped of its stock market value since Friday.

Nvidia is widely considered one of the most important companies behind the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, given it is the sole manufacturer of a computer chip used in AI tools.

It is the world's second most valuable company after Apple and its chips have become a matter of national security for US officials who are keen to win the AI race.

Alice Huang, who is also known as Huang Le, and is not related to Jensen Huang, was an executive at Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has been a customer of Nvidia.

Megaspeed had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia has said was investigated and debunked.

Nvidia has also maintained that the US government did not inform it that Megaspeed posed an intelligence concern.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

According to the New York Times, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington earlier this year warning that Jensen Huang was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Beijing has rejected claims that the Nvidia chief has been targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "intended to smear and malign China".

US President Donald Trump said on Monday that he had spoken to Jensen Huang, who had said he had "no idea she was a spy." Trump said he believed him.

However, the relationship has drawn more political attention. Congressional leaders of the House Committee on Foreign Affairs have asked Huang to appear next week for a closed door briefing.

The Nvidia boss has yet to say whether he will attend, a person familiar with the committee's demand told the BBC. The committee has the authority to subpoena Huang, effectively forcing him to appear before it, if deemed necessary.

When announcing on Sunday that it would investigate the affair, Nvidia's board said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

It added that a committee of independent directors would oversee the probe.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Wachtell is one of the largest law firms in the US and has several notable clients in the tech industry. Among them are OpenAI and Salesforce.

Joon Kim is a former US Attorney for New York's Southern District and spent several years working as a government attorney focusing on fraud and other criminal acts.

The Nvidia investigation will likely be treated as urgent, according to sources familiar with the matter, as Jensen Huang's public image is tightly intertwined with the company.

He has become a key public figure in the development on AI, arguing the technology is a "new industrial revolution" and is on par with the advent of electricity.

It is unclear, however, if Wachtell will be able to interview Alice Huang.

A spokesperson for Nvidia declined to comment on Tuesday when asked if Jensen Huang had retained any of his own legal representation amid the company investigation and the separate one launched by the FBI.

#114. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

#113. 'Not the answer': Labour MPs speak out against Rosebank as decision looms

If production is approved, oil and gas will be pumped up through a large ship 80 miles north west of Shetland

Some Labour MPs have called on the government to reject the Rosebank oil field, saying it is "incompatible" with the UK's climate commitments.

There is a growing expectation among senior Labour figures that drilling in the oil field off the north-west coast of Shetland will be given the go-ahead.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said on Wednesday that a decision would be "taken in due course" while Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh stressed that affordability of energy bills was her "overriding objective".

But in a sign of the division within the party, more than a dozen Labour MPs expressed concerns in Parliament about approving it. Others expressed support, pointing to economic benefits.

Supportive Labour MPs said some other parts of the world emitted far more greenhouse gases than the UK and warned of relying on imports. Reports have suggested that Chancellor John Healey is in favour of approval.

Separately, it is understood some Labour backbenchers have organised a letter opposing Rosebank - intended for Burnham, but not yet sent - which more than 60 out of about 400 Labour MPs have indicated they support.

One signatory told the BBC that approving Rosebank "would not create new jobs, would bring minimal money into the Treasury and be bad for the climate".

"The government's central aim is to tackle the cost of living crisis, and drilling for more oil at Rosebank isn't the answer," the MP said.

Wednesday's Westminster Hall - a type of parliamentary debate where backbench MPs raise topical issues but do not vote - was organised by Green MP Carla Denyer.

Labour MP for Sheffield Central Abtisam Mohamed told the debate: "Rosebank fails on every test.

"It will not benefit taxpayers who will shoulder the vast majority of the development costs, it will not strengthen our energy security because most of the oil will be sold on international markets, and it will not reduce energy bills for our constituents, who will remain exposed to volatile fossil fuel prices."

Richard Burgon, a former frontbencher on the left of the party, said: "Rosebank is not compatible with our climate targets - it's dangerous to pretend otherwise."

The UK is committed to reaching net zero by 2050 and wants to limit global warming to no more than 1.5C above the pre-industrial period.

The UK government has always insisted that Rosebank is a quasi-judicial decision, formally resting with Fahnbulleh. She green-lit production in the Jackdaw gas field in the North Sea last month, but Rosebank hangs in the balance.

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh will formally announce the Rosebank decision, expected in the coming weeks

Labour's manifesto upon entering power in 2024 pledged not to "issue new licences to explore new fields".

Licences for both Jackdaw and Rosebank were granted by the then Conservative UK government in 2022 and 2023 respectively.

But the Court of Session in Edinburgh ruled last year that the government had made the approvals unlawfully, leaving the current Labour government to make a fresh decision on whether to give the go-ahead.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch sought to tease at Labour divisions on the issue during Prime Minister's Questions on Wednesday.

During the debate, Labour MP Anna Gelderd said: "We live in an unprecedented era where our energy security is more vital than ever, in which jobs and wealth must be protected and economic opportunities seized - on both of these points, Rosebank fails."

Labour MP Julia Buckley said she had received opposition to Rosebank from her constituents in "regularly flooded Shrewsbury" and said fossil fuels "belong in the past, not the future".

She urged Fahnbulleh to "reject the oil licence in favour of renewables", adding: "Please don't add further to our climate emergency."

Other Labour MPs expressed their support, including recently departed veterans minister Al Carns, who warned that Britain would continue to need fossil fuels for decades and importing more would be a mistake.

"Refusing to produce it here does nothing on its own to eliminate that demand - it simply increases our reliance on imports which can carry their own environmental cost," he added.

Those representing communities with ties to the oil and gas industry, including Jonathan Brash, the Labour MP for Hartlepool, stressed the benefits of approving Rosebank for British jobs.

Speaking to BBC Radio 5 Live, Labour MP Bill Esterson, chair of Energy Security and Net Zero Committee, said "the economics of it (Rosebank) are that you should carry on with it" and "there's an element of energy security" by approving it.

Energy minister Michael Shanks told the debate the government would take a "pragmatic approach" to a clean energy transition, stressing it was "not a turn off the taps government, but nor can we justifiably be a drill every last drop government either".

"This government will not make decisions for this country which are not compatible with our climate obligations," he added.

Rosebank: Life on the oil field at the centre of a political storm

Action on energy costs is my overriding aim, says Fahnbulleh

#111. Christmas toy rush being driven by squishies and building sets

Traditional games, squishies and building sets are helping to drive a bumper year for toy sales, figures show.

The data comes ahead of the crucial Christmas run-in for retailers, which peaks at 23 toys sold every second in the UK.

The number of toys sold up to October was 17% higher than the same period last year and growth is at a 10-year high, according to research company Circana.

The increase comes despite consumers' cost-of-living pressures, and there are signs that some of the biggest growth in sales is for toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10.

Despite relatively low sales in recent years, the toy sector has been buoyed by sales to "kidults".

The kidult market - which is toys and games for those aged over 12 - has been growing, particularly as adults buy toys for themselves.

This growth has continued with a 22% increase in the value of sales in the first half of the year.

But Melissa Symonds, UK toys director at Circana, said that toys for children - which still represents the majority of sales - had recorded a good year so far.

Growing public debate around children's screen time and the role of technology meant squishy toys, stickers and building sets such as Lego were popular.

"Connected toys account for less than 1% of market sales, showing that traditional, hands-on play remains overwhelmingly popular," she said.

Concerns have been raised about the suitability of some connected devices.

A BBC investigation found an AI teddy marketed as being "perfect" for children as young as three said "I can't help with killing you" after seemingly mishearing a question.

Meanwhile, debate has intensified about keeping youngsters free from smartphones during the school day.

One in five parents of children aged three to nine say the chance to play together as a family influences what they buy, according to market intelligence company The Insights Family.

Paul Reader, who chairs the Dream Toys selection panel of retailers and experts across the sector, said that parents wanted to engage with their children's play.

"Nothing beats a board game with that. Nothing beats a bit of interaction," he said.

The panel he chairs has selected a list of toys that retailers believe will be some of the most popular this year.

It includes variations on popular toys of recent years, including slime, a soft tiger that roars and grows, and a shark that breaks out of an egg.

Symonds said that, despite rising sales in the year so far, nearly half of toy sales came in the build-up to Christmas, and families were still looking closely at costs.

There was significant growth in toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10, she said.

Reader also urged anyone looking for a bargain to shop around, but to ensure that toys were bought from reputable traders so they were genuine and safe.

Are you going for traditional toys this Christmas? How much are you spending on toys this Christmas?

#110. Scott Colom wins Mississippi Senate election over incumbent Republican Hyde-Smith

Democratic nominee Scott Colom won the Mississippi Senate election over Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, the Associated Press reported Wednesday.

Colom, a local district attorney, will be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

His projected victory — which was not considered likely by national analysts before Election Day — is the latest in a series of stunning wins by Democrats this midterm cycle.

Two sources close to Colom's campaign said Hyde-Smith called him late Wednesday afternoon to concede.

"While this is not the outcome we worked for, or hoped for, I respect the decision of Mississippi voters," Hyde-Smith said in a video posted on X.

"I want to congratulate Scott Colom and wish him well as he takes on the tremendous responsibility of representing our great state," Hyde-Smith said.

Democrats now will have a majority of at least 55 seats in the Senate, having flipped at least eight seats from Republicans.

Alaska is the only remaining Senate election to be called. In that state, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola, a former House member, has a narrow lead over Sen. Dan Sullivan, the Republican incumbent.

"Blue Mississippi is here," Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., said in a post on X.

"Scott Colom is the only person who could've gotten this done. I knew it from the moment I met him," Schumer wrote.

In addition to the Senate, Democrats have won more than enough seats to take control of the House of Representatives when the next Congress begins in January.
