#108. Poundland set for more time to secure a rescue deal as emergency talks continue

Poundland looks set to avoid immediate collapse after its US owners told MPs on Wednesday they were in favour of extending a deadline to find a rescue deal.

The discount chain, which has 11,000 employees, has been edging towards administration after being blocked by Gordon Borthers from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

A deadline to secure a buyer for the business is set to expire at midnight, but sources close to the situation told the BBC they expect more time will be given, thanks to the release of Â£10m in funding.

A committee of MPs questioned Gordon Brothers following accusations that the investment firm hopes to profit from selling off Poundland's 600 stores and stock piecemeal.

Gordon Brothers - which bought the chain for just Â£1 last year - is accused of forcing that outcome by restricting Poundland's access to available cash needed to restock stores and pay wages and rent, ahead of the crucial Christmas trading period.

The investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claims.

But pressure remains on Poundland's owners after a takeover bid was rejected. The decision prompted calls for answers from politicians, including Prime Minister Andy Burnham.

About Â£50m had been frozen by Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning Poundland bosses were unable to restock shelves across its 600 shops.

However, sources close to the matter told the BBC on Tuesday that a release of Â£10m by its owners, while "small in the scale of things" could help at least postpone the chain's collapse while a longer term solution was explored.

Following the meeting between MPs on the business and trade committee and representatives of Gordon Brothers, chairman Liam Byrne told the BBC he was "deeply concerned" and described the firm's behaviour as "much like a wolf on Wall Street, on the British High Street".

"They refused to prioritise, protecting jobs and stores over and above their own financial returns," he added.

"It's clear that they've taken millions out of Poundland at a time when they themselves said the business was on a knife edge."

Byrne said the committee's message to Gordon Brothers was to ensure Poundland had "access to the funds that it needs to trade at what should be one of the busiest periods in the year".

He urged Gordon Brothers to "do what is right to save jobs and stores and do that now".

"It's a good business, it's a much-loved business, and particularly in the middle of the cost of living crisis, communities up and down the country rely on access to the kind of stores that it operates," Byrne added.

Following the private meeting with MPs, Poundland's chief executive Barry Williams told the BBC the chain's board would hold an urgent meeting on Wednesday night.

The 10-day moratorium to protect Poundland from its creditors expires on Thursday, but if there is a prospect of a solvent deal, the deadline could be extended.

Views on the chain's performance differ between those running operations and the owners.

Poundland boss Williams told staff last week that like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4%. The sales period included Halloween, but Williams said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He added the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year where there were losses of Â£68m.

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It has argued the best way of securing jobs may be to split up the business and sell chunks of its 600 sites to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up Poundland stores which could mean continuing employment for workers in the event the chain is broken up.

The main issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland as a national chain.

#106. Some under-25s should get less benefits, Milburn review to say

Alan Milburn will publish his review next week

Some young people could see reductions to the benefits they are entitled to, under a radical overhaul of the system to be recommended by a government-commissioned review.

Alan Milburn's year-long report, which will be published on Tuesday, will recommend moving under-25s off the main sickness and disability benefits, the Times reported, external.

Milburn, a former Labour health secretary, has been tasked by the government with helping reduce the one in eight 18- to 24-year-olds in the UK who are not in education, employment or training - or "Neet".

He said on Wednesday night that his plans would not be "pain-free" and would affect both existing and new claimants.

His report is expected to recommend that under-25s are no longer able to claim the health element of universal credit or personal independence payment (Pip).

Instead, they would receive an allowance on the same basic rate as universal credit.

Some could be eligible for a new "additional cost allowance" to cover day-to-day costs related to their conditions.

But many claimants would receive less under the plans, which the government is set to take forward into legislation in the new year.

Milburn, a Labour MP from 1992 to 2010, is also expected to recommend an investment of billions into more employment support for young people, including work placements, job subsidies and one-to-one personal advisers.

Those with more severe disabilities and unable to work would get a new benefit paid at a higher rate.

Speaking to the Times about his report, Milburn said: "Candidly, it could mean that some people will get less financial support at the beginning of their benefit claim than under the current system.

"But actually, for young people, there's a different debate that we've got to resolve, which is whether the priority is their longer-term life chances or just their immediate shorter-term finances.

"I think what the current system does [is] it prioritises the latter over the former. I believe that's the wrong choice and it needs to be the other way around."

Just under one million young people in the UK are Neets, data from the Office for National Statistics (ONS) from April to June suggests. This was a rise of 3% on the same period last year.

Milburn's recommendations are likely to be backed by Prime Minister Andy Burnham but could prove controversial among Labour MPs, who opposed welfare reform under his predecessor Sir Keir Starmer.

The reforms would represent a radical overhaul of Britain's welfare system, at a time when there is heavy political pressure for the UK to find the money to spend more on defence.

If Milburn's recommendations were approved, it could take almost a decade for the British state to see monetary savings.

The new system "will need significant investment" and would involve "rebuilding the system from scratch," Milburn added.

He said that currently "you've got hundreds of thousands of young people stuck in a benefit system that is trapping them in poverty, giving them no prospect of prosperity or a job".

His new proposed system would involve an element of carrot and stick, where young people could lose some of their benefits if they did not engage with the extra work support options.

"A deal isn't a deal unless there are potential consequences," Milburn said. "Entitlements and responsibilities are two sides of the same coin."

A government spokesperson said: "We will always ensure there is a safety net for the most vulnerable and those who are unable to work.

"But the prime minister has been clear that we need a wholesale change in the way we support young people to grow up in this country, where we invest in their success rather than paying for failure."

One in eight young people still out of work or education ahead of major review

#103. Jensen Huang affair in D.C.: Resignation calls and moves to tighten Nvidia China exports

Members of Congress are calling on Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang to resign, and momentum is building to subpoena the executive, whose company is a leader in advanced artificial intelligence chips.

Senate banking committee member Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., doubled down Wednesday on his call for Huang to resign, and a key Democratic House oversight panel member and House Republican piled on.

"As a matter of his own conscience, he should resign," Cramer told CNBC, in response to revelations that Huang had an affair with an alleged Chinese spy as the chipmaker he co-founded was given permission from the U.S. government to sell its technology to China.

Huang, who leads a company that's been a chief beneficiary of the artificial intelligence boom, on Sunday admitted to having an affair with a woman whom news outlets have reported U.S. intelligence concluded was a Chinese spy. The revelation is sending shockwaves through Washington, with lawmakers now fearing that U.S. AI dominance could be at risk.

"What bothers me at the end of it is what we don't know is what she knows about him that can be utilized," Cramer said, referring to Alice Huang and Jensen Huang, who are not related. "This is a guy that's right now so vulnerable to extortion, blackmail. I just don't think, as a national security matter, we can run the risk."

Cramer's comments come on the deadline set by a trio of House lawmakers who requested Huang to testify before their committees on Nov. 18.

GOP Reps. Brian Mast, chair of the House Foreign Affairs Committee; John Moolenaar, chair of the Permanent Select Committee on China; and Rick Crawford, who leads the House intelligence panel, called for Huang to appear in a Nov. 4 letter. Congress members in the majority party also have subpoena power, should they choose to use it.

Mast and Moolenaar told reporters on Wednesday afternoon that they had not yet heard from Huang and that they were giving him until the end of the day to respond.

Asked whether the committees were considering subpoenaing Huang if he doesn't agree to appear, Mast said "yes."

Moolenaar, when asked the same question, said "all three committees have subpoena power, and I know all three committees have a very serious interest in getting to the bottom of this and having transparency around this."

"We are going to continue to pursue this," Moolenaar said.

The lawmakers are far from alone in their criticism, concern and outright shock over the affair. And the scrutiny is only expected to intensify with Democrats poised to take over both chambers of Congress in January. Huang has been close with President Donald Trump during the president's second term, traveling on Air Force One to China in May.

"Jensen Huang should resign from Nvidia," Rep. Suhas Subramanyam, D-Va., a member of the House Oversight Committee, told CNBC. Subramanyam is the top Democrat on the panel's Subcommittee on Military and Foreign Affairs.

"I didn't agree with his sentiment that there's no downside to exporting chips to China," he said. "But I never thought that he might be having an affair with a Chinese spy who was helping smuggle chips into China at an alarming rate."

Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., who is retiring from Congress after sometimes bucking Trump, also said the CEO should step down.

"From the little I know, yes." Bacon told CNBC in response to whether Huang should resign. "I mean, he allowed himself to get compromised by the Chinese government. Not good."

Huang was notably opposed to strict export controls on AI chips to China, which ruffled feathers with China hawks on Capitol Hill.

Democrats are widely expected to launch myriad investigations into the White House and industry when they assume control of both chambers of Congress in January. Nvidia is now likely to be at the center of those probes.

"There's two parts to this investigation that [need] to happen somewhere immediately," Subramanyam said. "One is what was going on with Nvidia and China, and two is why the administration did nothing about it even though they appeared to have known, and what the administration knew, and what influence the money from Nvidia and Jensen Huang had on this administration, essentially staying silent."

Rep. Jay Obernolte, R-Calif., co-chair of the House AI task force, said Tuesday he hopes that the Huang scandal can avoid becoming a distraction in talks about imposing export controls on advanced AI chips.

The situation may give Congress additional ammunition as it tries to pass a suite of bills in this year's National Defense Authorization Act to tighten export controls on advanced AI chips.

Congress has still not passed the annual defense bill, which is a top priority for lawmakers in the lame-duck session.

Those bills include the AI Overwatch Act, the MATCH Act and the Chip Security Act — all of which are aimed at preventing U.S. chip technology from falling into adversarial hands.

Sens. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., and Jim Banks, R-Ind., in a statement following the revelation of Huang's affair, urged their colleagues to include the AI Overwatch Act. Warren, the incoming Senate banking committee chair, sponsored the bill in the Senate with Banks.

"Huang's confirmation of a relationship with a suspected spy for China's intelligence service makes these national security concerns even more urgent and raises important questions NVIDIA must answer about the extent to which these activities were related," the senators said. "It is time for Congress to take decisive action to pass key bills like the AI Overwatch Act in the NDAA that will cut off pathways through which China accesses advanced chips."

And Subramanyam warned that what Nvidia does now — and whether Huang remains atop the company — could affect its relationship with Washington and whether lawmakers crack down.

"If Nvidia finds out that he knew about what was going on and does nothing about it, then Nvidia is making a choice and us as regulators and as watchdogs need to defend the American people," he said.

#99. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#96. Nvidia prepares to oust Jensen Huang, WSJ reports — stock sinks

Shares of Nvidia fell more than 9% in after-hours trading on Wednesday after The Wall Street Journal reported that the company's board is preparing to oust CEO Jensen Huang.

The news landed minutes after The New York Times reported that Huang had an extramarital relationship with another executive at the company. Nvidia's board is preparing to meet as soon as Wednesday to determine Huang's future at the company, according to the Journal.

The alleged relationship was with Kimberly Powell, vice president of health care at Nvidia, according to the Times' report. Powell's bio on the Nvidia website says she's "responsible for the company's worldwide healthcare business, including hardware and software platforms for accelerated computing, AI, and visualization that power the ecosystem of medical imaging, life sciences, drug discovery, and healthcare analytics."

Huang and Powell were reportedly captured on camera being affectionate with one another at a hotel in San Jose, California in March, according to the Times.

Nvidia and Powell did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment. Heller, the crisis PR firm Huang reportedly hired as early as last year, did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

The report comes days after Huang admitted to an affair with a woman who is believed by U.S. intelligence of being a Chinese spy. The CEO was romantically involved with Alice Huang, according to The Financial Times, who U.S. intelligence officials believe is a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, The New York Times reported.

Nvidia's board of directors has opened an independent investigation and hired the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to look into the spying allegations.

"I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," Huang wrote in the message to Nvidia staff on Sunday. "I am deeply sorry."

Most of Huang's Nvidia shares are co-owned with his wife, Lori Huang, and are held in trusts. About 800 million shares are held by those trusts. Huang holds about 70 million shares on his own and has limited economic control over the company.

Rumors of an affair between Huang and Powell have circulated for years, in part because Powell lacks a background in health or science, according to a former employee who asked not to be named because of the sensitivity of the matter.

Healthcare is a smaller business at Nvidia. The company makes chips for medical devices and MRI machines, but they are a minor in comparison to the revenue generated from its advanced AI systems and graphics processing units, or GPUs. In recent appearances, including at the company's annual GTC conference, Powell has spoken about how Nvidia's AI technology can help accelerate drug discovery, assist providers and improve clinical efficiency. Powell has been leading the group since 2017.

Nvidia's stock is down 7% this week and is on pace for its sixth straight weekly slide, reflecting broad concern on the sustainability of the artificial intelligence buildout. The AI infrastructure trade was hit particularly hard last week after the midterm elections, as Democrats won sweeping majorities in both houses of Congress, and dozens of ballot measures past that could make building data centers more difficult.

Huang has become synonymous with the AI industry, and he is often referred to by his first name ,"Jensen," by customers and rivals. Nvidia has been one of the primary beneficiaries of the AI boom since its chips are used to train models and run large AI workloads.

—CNBC's Lora Kolodny and Kif Leswing contributed to this report
